_tool_result:_
```
1	# 229 — Data box: a language-agnostic file source (and a paired file sink)
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	The `data` kind is recognized by the schema (`call` and `data` are
9	the two valid kinds) and accepted by the loader, but it has no
10	runtime semantics distinct from `call`. It carries a `ref` and an
11	`fn` like a call box and runs through whichever language driver
12	matches the file extension. The only difference is the green badge
13	on the canvas — pure cosmetic.
14	
15	A user reading the editor reasonably expects `data` to mean "read
16	some data, emit it" — "if it walks like a duck…" — but the current
17	implementation doesn't deliver that. `libs/files.lua::read_text`
18	covers the actual "read a text file" need (issue 216, complete) via
19	a regular call box.
20	
21	## Intended behavior
22	
23	Repurpose the `data` kind into a **language-agnostic file source**.
24	A data box has:
25	- `ref` — an absolute or map-relative path to a file on disk.
26	- **No `fn`** — the dispatch layer is the function.
27	- **No inputs** — emits whenever its successors fire.
28	- A single output wire carrying the file's contents.
29	
30	The runtime reads the file at the dispatch layer, encodes the bytes
31	as a string (UTF-8), and pushes the value onto the output wire. No
32	language driver is invoked. Same shape as the iterator box (issue
33	221): kind says "call," presence of a routing field marks the box
34	as a primitive the dispatch layer handles directly.
35	
36	### Why language-agnostic matters
37	
38	A user writing a Bash function that needs file contents shouldn't
39	have to wire through a Lua read box — and vice versa. A file's
40	bytes are a string; every language consumes a string; the
41	read-and-emit step has no language-specific anything. Pulling it
42	into the dispatch layer makes it available identically from every
43	language.
44	
45	It also keeps the graph readable: a user reading a map sees
46	`config.json → parse → ...` and immediately knows where the data
47	came from. The current alternative — a call box pointing at
48	`files.read_text` — works but adds a layer of indirection that
49	buries what the user is doing.
50	
51	### Paired sink: `kind: "sink"` (or similar) for writing files
52	
53	The complement is a language-agnostic file sink. A user wires a
54	string into it; the dispatch layer writes the string to the
55	configured path. Inputs:
56	- `path` (string) — destination on disk
57	- `text` (string) — content to write
58	
59	No outputs (per issue 226's sink semantics). No `ref` or `fn`. The
60	kind name needs picking — `sink` is taken metaphorically already;
61	`file_write` is unambiguous; `write` is short. Defer the bikeshed.
62	
63	Both the source and the sink atomic-write where it matters: write
64	to `path.tmp`, fsync, rename to `path`. Standard pattern; matches
65	how `soramech-data` handles its writes.
66	
67	### Schema
68	
69	For the source:
70	```json
71	{
72	  "id": "config",
73	  "kind": "data",
74	  "path": "config.json",
75	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
76	}
77	```
78	
79	`ref` is gone — repurposed as `path` so the field name says what it
80	is at the new abstraction level (read a file at this path, not call
81	a function at this ref). Inputs are absent; outputs are absent (the
82	single-output rule still gives one wire).
83	
84	For the sink, the kind name TBD; data layout:
85	```json
86	{
87	  "id": "save",
88	  "kind": "file_write",
89	  "inputs": [
90	    {"name": "path",  "type": "string"},
91	    {"name": "text",  "type": "string"}
92	  ],
93	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
94	}
95	```
96	
97	No `path` field on the box — it's wired in. Lets the user compute
98	paths upstream (e.g. by a function that builds a timestamped name).
99	Could also add an optional `path` field for the static case where
100	the user just wants a file written to a fixed location.
101	
102	### Concerns about language-agnostic file IO
103	
104	The original "data files have language semantics" concern (paraphrasing
105	from prior conversations: "different languages store strings
106	differently") doesn't apply here. The runtime owns the read/write,
107	not any language; it always emits bytes-as-UTF-8 string and always
108	accepts the same shape on the way in. Languages each receive a
109	plain string from the wire and turn it into whatever their native
110	string type is — that conversion is already part of the language
111	spec contract (issue 303).
112	
113	There is one wrinkle: **binary data**. UTF-8 encoding of arbitrary
114	bytes can fail (invalid sequences). Two ways to handle:
115	1. Reject non-UTF-8 files at read time with a clear error. Forces
116	   the user to acknowledge the binary case explicitly.
117	2. Pass through as raw bytes in some marker (base64?) and let the
118	   downstream box decode. Adds complexity most users won't need.
119	
120	Recommendation: option 1 for now. Binary data is out of scope
121	until someone needs it; the error surfaces clearly so the user
122	can switch to a language-specific binary reader.
123	
124	### JSON parsing
125	
126	The user asked whether parsing should happen in the data box or in
127	a downstream box. Recommendation: **the data box is text-only**;
128	JSON parsing belongs to a downstream call (Lua: `dkjson.decode`,
129	language-agnostic option: a parse box). Reasons:
130	- Parsing a JSON file produces a structured object, not a string;
131	  the wire protocol between boxes is currently strings (per the
132	  language spec contract). Moving structured values across the
133	  wire is a separate change with bigger implications.
134	- Different downstream uses want different parsings (decoded
135	  table, JSON path extraction, schema validation). Letting the
136	  data box stay narrow keeps it composable.
137	
138	## Migration
139	
140	Existing `data` boxes (if any) currently behave like `call`. They
141	have a `ref` and `fn`. Two options:
142	1. Convert `data` → `call` for old boxes, then redefine `data` as
143	   the new file-source shape.
144	2. Leave old `data` boxes as-is; the editor refuses to interpret
145	   them as the new shape until the user explicitly converts.
146	
147	Option 1 is cleaner since the existing usage was incidental
148	(nobody was treating `data` as semantically distinct). A small
149	migration in `src/003-loader.lua` rewrites old `data` boxes on
150	read.
151	
152	## Suggested implementation sequence
153	
154	1. **Schema** (`src/001-schema.lua`): allow `data` boxes with
155	   `path` and no `ref`/`fn`; the new kind for writes (TBD name)
156	   takes inputs `path` and `text` and no `ref`/`fn`.
157	2. **Loader** (`src/003-loader.lua`): convert legacy `data` boxes
158	   (`kind=data` with `ref`/`fn`) to `kind=call`. Validate the new
159	   shapes.
160	3. **Phase 2 executor** (`src/004-executor.lua`): handle the new
161	   kinds directly — read the file and push to output for the
162	   source; receive inputs and write to disk for the sink. No
163	   driver invocation either way.
164	4. **Phase 3 dispatch** (issue 304): same handling at the dispatch
165	   layer. Source and sink are dispatch-layer primitives, alongside
166	   iterator and comparator routing.
167	5. **Inspector** (`assets/js/004-inspector.js`): when `kind=data`,
168	   show a `path` text input in place of `ref`/`fn`/inputs. When
169	   `kind=file_write` (or final name), show the inputs section as
170	   usual but no ref/fn/output.
171	6. **Box rendering** (`assets/js/002-boxes.js`): keep the green
172	   badge for the source; pick a color for the sink.
173	7. **Documentation**: update `docs/001-architecture.md` to describe
174	   the new `data` semantics. The old "library box" notes (if any)
175	   need a small rewrite.
176	
177	## Open questions
178	
179	- **Sink kind name**: `file_write`, `write`, `out`, `save`,
180	  `to_file`, `sink`. Lean toward `file_write` for "says what it is."
181	- **Static vs wired path on the sink**: support both, or pick one?
182	  Wired path is more flexible; static path is a common case worth
183	  one fewer wire. Probably accept both — `path` in the box JSON
184	  is used as the default; if a wire is connected to the `path`
185	  input, it overrides.
186	- **Binary data**: explicitly out of scope per above; revisit when
187	  a real use case appears.
188	- **Atomic-write vs simple write**: atomic-write (`tmp + rename`)
189	  is the safe default. If a user wants log-append semantics,
190	  that's a different sink kind (or a flag on this one).
191	
192	## Relevant files
193	
194	- `src/001-schema.lua` — kind validation, new shape
195	- `src/003-loader.lua` — legacy `data` migration
196	- `src/004-executor.lua` — phase 2 runtime handling
197	- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — kind-specific inspector layout
198	- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — render
199	- `docs/001-architecture.md` — update box-kinds section
200	- `issues/completed/216-read-file-box.md` — Lua-side equivalent
201	  that the language-agnostic source supersedes for primary use
202	- `issues/304-task-dispatch-layer.md` — phase 3 home for these
203	  primitives, alongside iterator and comparator
204	- `issues/completed/109-data-files-persistent-and-ephemeral.md`
205	  — soramech-data is a separate concern; this issue does not
206	  affect it
207	
```

```
1	# 224 — Editable port names and canvas-side value editing
2	
3	## Status
4	complete (canvas-overlay reverted; name editing in inspector,
5	value moved below name+variadic row)
6	
7	## Scope changed twice
8	
9	**First pass** (canvas-overlay version): moved the editable name
10	input out of the inspector and onto a DOM overlay floating next to
11	the box's input dot.
12	
13	**Why that was wrong**:
14	1. The `<input>` overlay captured pointer events on the box body;
15	   users dragging a box from near a port dot landed on the field
16	   instead and entered text-edit mode.
17	2. The overlay text was frozen at one screen size — it didn't
18	   scale with zoom.
19	
20	**Final design**: keep names on the canvas as plain `ctx.fillText`
21	labels (scale with zoom, ignore clicks). Names are also editable in
22	the inspector — same identity displayed in two places, so the user
23	sees what's there at a glance and can edit when the inspector is
24	open. The inspector port row now spans two lines: name + variadic
25	toggle on top, value on a wider second line below, so the value
26	(often the longest entry) gets the full sidebar width.
27	
28	## Implementation notes
29	
30	`assets/js/004-inspector.js::mk_port_display` rebuilt:
31	- Top row: name `<input>` + optional `:type` span + variadic button.
32	- Bottom row: full-width value `<input>`.
33	- Variadic-slot names disable the name input — variadic ops own
34	  the `<base>_<N>` pattern, and freeform renames break the group's
35	  membership tracking. The disabled state visually softens the
36	  field (no border, dim color) so it reads as "managed by the
37	  toggle" rather than "broken."
38	- Name commit on blur / Enter; Escape reverts to the saved name.
39	
40	`004-inspector.js` exports `rename_port(box, port_idx, new_name)`:
41	- Validation: non-empty, identifier-shaped (`^[A-Za-z_]\w*$`),
42	  unique within the box.
43	- Rewrites `to_input` on every wire targeting the port (both
44	  endpoints, mutating in place per the stale-cache lesson from 217).
45	- Re-renders the inspector if the renamed box is the one on
46	  display.
47	
48	`002-boxes.js::draw_box` keeps the canvas-rendered port name label
49	(it was briefly removed during the overlay experiment).
50	
51	`assets/js/009-box-overlays.js` and the matching CSS removed; the
52	overlay layer is gone.
53	
54	### Edge cases
55	
56	- **Variadic slots can't be renamed in-inspector** — the field is
57	  disabled. Toggle the group off if you need to break out of the
58	  pattern.
59	- **Renamed port had a wire**: the wire's `to_input` rewrites on
60	  both endpoints; the canvas re-renders with the new label without
61	  losing the connection.
62	- **Typo'd a name to something the function doesn't expect**: this
63	  is the intentional tradeoff from issue 208. The runtime error
64	  surfaces clearly rather than silently skipping.
65	
66	## Current behavior
67	
68	Input port names are read-only labels in the inspector — derived from
69	the function's parameter names when the file browser populates the
70	box (issue 207, 208). Each port row in the inspector also carries a
71	literal value input. With variadic inputs (issue 217), a box with
72	several slots produces a tall, narrow list of name+type+value rows
73	that crowds the sidebar.
74	
75	The canvas already renders port names next to their dots, so the
76	name appears twice — once on the canvas (read-only label) and once
77	in the inspector (read-only label).
78	
79	## Intended behavior
80	
81	Move both the port name and the literal value input out of the
82	inspector and onto the canvas next to the port dot. The inspector
83	keeps only the variadic toggle / remove-slot buttons and any
84	non-port box-level fields (ref / fn / comparator).
85	
86	### Why
87	
88	- The inspector is crowded with redundant data (the canvas already
89	  shows port names).
90	- Literal values often need more horizontal room than a 72px input
91	  field allows, especially for prompts, paths, or JSON snippets.
92	- Reading the box's full configuration without clicking through the
93	  inspector becomes possible — every input's name and value is
94	  visible directly on the canvas.
95	- 208's "read-only port names" decision was about preventing typo
96	  drift between source code and box JSON; the file browser still
97	  populates names from the parsed signature, but the user should be
98	  free to override them after the fact.
99	
100	### Reverses 208's read-only-name decision
101	
102	Issue 208 made input port names non-editable on the principle that
103	they should match the parsed function signature exactly. This issue
104	flips that — names become editable again, with the trade-off that a
105	typo can desynchronize the editor's record from the function the box
106	calls. The cost is a per-box runtime error rather than a hidden
107	silent skip, so it surfaces clearly. The benefit is that users can
108	rename inputs freely (renaming a Lua function's parameter and then
109	updating the box does not require re-browsing).
110	
111	### Layout
112	
113	Each input port row on the canvas becomes:
114	
115	```
116	●  text_0    ┌──────────────────┐
117	             │ "hello world"    │
118	             └──────────────────┘
119	```
120	
121	- The port dot stays on the box's left edge (unchanged).
122	- The port name is an editable text field, rendered as an HTML
123	  overlay positioned next to the dot. Pan and zoom adjust the
124	  overlay's transform to match the canvas.
125	- The literal value field is another HTML overlay below or to the
126	  right of the name.
127	
128	### HTML overlays over the canvas
129	
130	Canvas 2D doesn't do native text inputs, so the editable fields are
131	DOM `<input>` elements absolutely positioned over the canvas. A
132	small per-box overlay container holds them, transforms with the
133	camera (CSS transform applied on every render frame), and updates
134	the underlying `box.inputs[i]` on input.
135	
136	This is the same pattern as the source viewer (issue 215) — DOM
137	elements layered over the canvas, with positions computed from
138	`Canvas.world_to_screen`. The complexity is keeping overlay state
139	in sync with the canvas: they need to be created when a box is
140	loaded, destroyed when a box is deleted, repositioned every frame
141	(or when pan/zoom changes), and hidden when off-screen.
142	
143	### Inspector becomes lighter
144	
145	After this change the inspector shows:
146	- Box label (editable)
147	- ref / fn (with browse / view buttons)
148	- For each input: the variadic toggle (`var` / `var ×` / `×`) only.
149	  No name field, no value field — those live on the canvas.
150	- Output: the comparator toggle and comparand input (unchanged).
151	- Delete-box button (unchanged).
152	
153	Roughly half the height the current inspector takes for variadic
154	boxes.
155	
156	## Open questions
157	
158	- **Overlay performance**: dozens of overlays multiplied by frequent
159	  pan/zoom updates is a real cost. Tests are needed; a bounding-box
160	  cull (only render overlays for visible boxes) is the obvious
161	  optimization if it matters.
162	- **Value-input width**: the field grows with the input's content, or
163	  is a fixed width with horizontal scroll? Probably grow-on-content
164	  with a max width clamp, similar to what monospace text does.
165	- **Editing port names while wires are connected**: renaming a port
166	  while a wire is attached should rename the wire's `to_input` /
167	  `from_branch` on both endpoints atomically — same bilateral-update
168	  pattern the variadic ops use.
169	- **Name validation**: what's a valid port name? Probably `[A-Za-z_][\w]*`
170	  (a Lua / C / shell-compatible identifier). Reject names that start
171	  with digits, contain spaces, etc. Reject duplicates within a box.
172	- **Backward compat**: existing maps with the current layout work as
173	  before — no migration needed. The change is purely UI.
174	
175	## Suggested implementation sequence
176	
177	1. Create `assets/js/009-box-overlays.js` (new module) responsible
178	   for per-box DOM overlays. Public API: `update_overlays_for(box_id)`
179	   to create / refresh overlays for a box, `remove_overlays_for(box_id)`
180	   on box deletion, `redraw_overlays()` on pan/zoom.
181	2. Wire the overlays into the render loop: after `Boxes.draw_all`,
182	   call `redraw_overlays()` to reposition the overlays for the
183	   current camera. The overlays themselves don't redraw — only their
184	   `style.left` / `style.top` / `style.transform` updates.
185	3. Remove the name + value rendering from `mk_port_display` in
186	   `004-inspector.js`. Leave only the variadic controls.

188	   (the overlay's editable input replaces it).
189	5. Wire validation: name must be a valid identifier and unique
190	   within the box's `inputs`. Validate in the input's `oninput`
191	   handler; revert to the previous valid name on rejection.
192	6. Test against `maps/hello`, `maps/classify-demo`, and a fresh map
193	   with variadic inputs (e.g. via `libs/text.lua`'s concat).
194

1	# 212 — Editor interaction modes
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	complete
6	
7	## Current behavior
8	
9	Creating a box requires a double-click on the canvas. There is no dedicated
10	wire-erase tool; wires must be clicked individually to select them and then
11	deleted with the keyboard. Deleting a box that has incoming connections fails
12	with a server 409 error because the server checks for references before allowing
13	the delete.
14	
15	## Intended behavior
16	
17	Two tool-mode buttons appear in the top toolbar:
18	
19	**+ box**: clicking the button enters add-box mode (button highlights). The
20	next click on the canvas places a new box at the cursor position and returns
21	to select mode automatically.
22	
23	**✕ wire**: clicking the button enters erase-wire mode (button highlights).
24	While the mouse button is held down and the cursor is dragged across the canvas,
25	any wire the cursor passes over is immediately erased. Releasing the mouse button
26	exits erase-wire mode and returns to select mode.
27	
28	Double-click to create a box is removed; the + box tool is the only creation
29	path.
30	
31	Box deletion is unconditional: before calling the server DELETE, the client
32	strips all connection records referencing the box from every other box (PUT each
33	affected box), then issues the DELETE. The server's 409 reference guard is no
34	longer triggered. No confirmation dialog is shown.
35	
36	## Implementation notes
37	
38	The shipped behavior diverges from the original spec in one place:
39	
40	- **Box creation** is via the canvas right-click context menu ("add
41	  box here") rather than a dedicated `+ box` toolbar mode. One click
42	  instead of two, and consistent with how other context-driven
43	  actions in the editor work.
44	- **Wire erasure** is the dedicated `✕ wire` toolbar tool as
45	  originally specced — click the button, hold-and-drag across the
46	  canvas, wires under the cursor are erased.
47	- `tool_mode` has values `'select' | 'erase-wire'` (no `'add-box'`).
48	- **Box deletion** strips connection references from every other box
49	  client-side before issuing the server `DELETE`, avoiding the 409
50	  reference-guard error.
51	
52	`set_tool_mode(mode)` (in `005-app.js`) manages the active button
53	class and canvas cursor (`'cell'` for erase-wire). Erase-wire tracks
54	a Set of already-queued wire signatures per drag to avoid
55	double-deletes. Connections are removed from the local Boxes cache
56	immediately (for instant visual feedback) and then deleted from the
57	server asynchronously.
58	
59	## Suggested implementation steps
60	
61	1. `005-app.js` — add `tool_mode`, `set_tool_mode`, `wire_sig` helpers.
62	   Update mousedown/mousemove/mouseup handlers for new modes.
63	   Rewrite `delete_box` to strip references before deleting.
64	   Remove dblclick handler.
65	2. `index.html` — add `#tool-add-box` and `#tool-erase-wire` buttons to toolbar.
66	   Add `.toolbar-btn.active` CSS.
67	
68	## Related documents
69	
70	- assets/js/005-app.js — App closure, event handlers
71	- assets/index.html — toolbar HTML and CSS
72	
```

2	
3	## Status
4	
5	complete
6	
7	## Implementation notes
8	
9	Server: added `handle_list_src` (GET /maps/:name/src) and `handle_get_src`
10	(GET /maps/:name/src/:file) in src/005-http-server.lua.
11	
12	API: added `list_src_files()` and `get_src_file(filename)` in 003-api.js.
13	
14	Parser (007-filebrowser.js): Lua matches `function M.name(args)` for inputs;
15	scans function body for `return` statements and uses bare identifier names as
16	output port names (falls back to "result" for single complex expressions).
17	Bash matches `name() {` and scans for `local var="${N}"` to name positional
18	inputs; outputs always ["output"].
19	
20	Inspector (004-inspector.js): ref field is now a read-only display plus a
21	Browse button that replaces the inspector with the file browser. On function
22	click, ref/fn/inputs/outputs are set on the box and saved. Inputs and outputs
23	are shown as read-only port-display lists, not editable fields. Branch box
24	inputs remain manually editable (no source function to parse).
25	
26	## Architecture update (2026-05-07)
27	
28	The output port population step (deriving `box.outputs` from return statement
29	names) is removed as part of the single-wire output redesign (see issue 210).
30	When a function is selected via the file browser, only `box.inputs` is set —
31	`box.outputs` is no longer part of the call box schema.
32	
33	The file browser's return-statement parser (`parse_lua_returns`) is kept for
34	now but the results are no longer applied to the box. It may be removed in a
35	future cleanup pass once issue 210 is implemented.
36	
37	## Blockers
38	
39	- 201 (boxes must render for this to be testable)
40	
41	## Current behavior
42	
43	The inspector shows editable text inputs for ref, fn, inputs, and outputs.
44	Users must type port names by hand with no relationship to the actual function
45	being called. Mismatches between the box config and the function signature
46	cause silent runtime failures.
47	
48	## Intended behavior
49	
50	For call boxes:
51	- "ref" field shows current value plus a Browse button
52	- Clicking Browse replaces the inspector panel with a file list from the
53	  map's src/ directory
54	- Selecting a file shows all public functions parsed from that file, each
55	  with its inputs (from the function signature) and outputs (from return
56	  statements), displayed as read-only text
57	- Clicking a function sets ref and fn on the box, derives inputs and outputs
58	  from the parsed signature, saves the box, and returns to the normal
59	  inspector view
60	- Inputs and outputs in the inspector are read-only — not editable fields
61	
62	For branch boxes: no file browser; ports remain manually configured.
63	
64	## Suggested implementation steps
65	
66	1. Server: add GET /maps/:name/src (list files in src/) and
67	   GET /maps/:name/src/:file (return file content as text/plain)
68	2. API: add list_src_files() and get_src_file(filename)
69	3. New file 007-filebrowser.js: parse_functions(content, filename),
70	   render(container, on_select) that shows file list → function list
71	4. Inspector: replace ref text input with display + Browse button;
72	   remove editable inputs/outputs; show derived ports as read-only list
73	5. When function selected: set box.ref, box.fn, box.inputs, box.outputs
74	   (with type: "any" for all), autosave
75	
76	## Parser conventions
77	
78	Lua (.lua): match `function M.name(args)` for inputs; scan function body
79	for `return` statements and use variable names as output names. If the
80	return expression is not a bare identifier, name it "result".
81	
82	Bash (.sh): match `name() {`; scan body for `local var="${N}"` to name
83	positional inputs; output is always one value named "output".
84	
85	Other extensions: show file in list but display "no parser" when selected.

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 217 — Concat box with per-input variadic ports and auto-grow/shrink
2	
3	## Status
4	complete
5	
6	## Implementation notes
7	
8	**Part A** — `libs/text.lua` ships `M.concat(sep, ...)` and
9	`M.split(text, sep)`. Concat is variadic to match the editor's
10	variadic-input model. Split returns a Lua array; the driver shim
11	JSON-encodes for the single output wire. The accompanying
12	`libs/text.lua.info.md` documents both functions.
13	
14	**Part B** — Variadic UI lives in `assets/js/004-inspector.js`:
15	
16	- Helpers (`parse_variadic_name`, `is_variadic_slot`,
17	  `variadic_slots_for`, `last_variadic_index`) parse and query the
18	  `<base>_<index>` naming convention for variadic slots.
19	- `make_variadic(port_name)` toggles a non-variadic port into a
20	  one-slot variadic group (rename `text` → `text_0`, add base to
21	  `box.variadic_inputs`, update connection records on this box and
22	  every upstream box).
23	- `unmake_variadic(base)` collapses a group back to a single port
24	  (drop higher slots and their wires, rename slot 0 back to base).
25	- `add_variadic_slot(base)` appends `<base>_<last+1>`. Used by the
26	  auto-grow path; also reachable directly when needed.
27	- `remove_variadic_slot(slot_name)` drops a single slot and renames
28	  higher slots down by one, updating connections atomically per
29	  slot.
30	- `auto_grow_after_set(box, slot_name)` is the public-from-Inspector
31	  helper. If `slot_name` is the last slot of its variadic group, it
32	  appends a new empty slot. Idempotent — calling again on the same
33	  slot is a no-op once the slot is no longer last.
34	
35	The wire-create path (`006-wires.js::create_connection`) calls
36	`Inspector.auto_grow_after_set(dst_box, to_input)` after a
37	successful PUT. The inspector's per-port value-input handler also
38	calls it when a non-empty literal value is set on a slot. Together
39	these cover both growth triggers — wire-connect and value-set.
40	
41	Per-port UI (`mk_port_display`):
42	
43	- Plain port → `var` toggle (turn variadic on).
44	- First slot of a variadic group → `var ×` toggle (collapse back).
45	- Non-first variadic slot → `×` (remove this slot only; higher
46	  slots rename down).
47	- No `+` button — slots auto-grow. Wire-disconnect and
48	  value-clear do NOT shrink (only `×` removes structure).
49	
50	Schema accepts `variadic_inputs` as an optional array of strings
51	(`src/001-schema.lua`).
52	
53	Comparator toggle (`004-inspector.js::cmp_btn.onclick`) now severs
54	all outgoing wires on either toggle direction, fixing the prior
55	behavior where lt/eq/gt wires from a previous comparator state
56	silently re-appeared when the user re-enabled it.
57	
58	## Deliberate omissions / follow-ups
59	
60	- **Auto-shrink on wire-disconnect** is intentionally not done —
61	  the user explicitly chose "explicit `×` is the only way to shrink"
62	  so transient editing doesn't lose structure.
63	- **Editable port names** and **value editing on the canvas
64	  itself** belong in issue 224 — bigger redesign than 217 covers.
65	- **Future ideas** (language-spec-driven variadic detection,
66	  alternative variadic shapes for keyword args / streams /
67	  discriminated unions) are captured below.
68	
69	## Current behavior
70	No standard concat box exists. No mechanism exists for a box to have a variable
71	number of input wires on a single logical port.
72	
73	## Intended behavior
74	
75	### Part A — text library box
76	`libs/text.lua` exposes two functions:
77	
78	`M.concat(sep, ...)` — join N strings:
79	- `sep` — separator string (e.g. `"\n\n"`)
80	- `...` — any number of text values to join (variadic)
81	- returns a single `result` port
82	
83	The function is variadic so its `text` input can expand to any count without
84	modifying the source file.
85	
86	`M.split(text, sep)` — split one string into parts:
87	- `text` — the input string
88	- `sep` — the separator to split on (plain string, not a pattern)
89	- returns a single `result` port containing a JSON-encoded array of the parts
90	
91	Output ports are named at wire-design time, so a variable number of runtime
92	parts cannot map to separate wires. A JSON array on one port is the only
93	consistent design — the downstream box decodes it.
94	
95	Note: one output wire per box is a deliberate design constraint, not a missing
96	feature. Positional multi-output was explicitly rejected as fragile (multiple
97	return statements with the same tuple width are indistinguishable). The
98	comparator (lt/eq/gt branching) is the only multi-path mechanism. Whatever the
99	function returns goes down the single output wire as one value — arrays or JSON
100	objects if multiple values need to travel together.
101	
102	### Part B — per-input variadic flag
103	
104	Variadic is a property of a specific input port, not the box as a whole. A box
105	could have `sep` (fixed) and `text` (variadic). The inspector shows a toggle on
106	each input to mark it variadic.
107	
108	#### Data model
109	
110	The box JSON gains a `variadic_inputs` set (array of base names):
111	```json
112	{
113	  "inputs": ["sep", "text_0", "text_1", "text_2"],
114	  "variadic_inputs": ["text"]
115	}
116	```
117	
118	Slots of a variadic input are stored as `<base>_<index>` in the `inputs` array.
119	The executor already passes all inputs positionally — no executor changes needed.
120	Connections reference slots by their indexed name (`"text_0"`, `"text_1"`, etc.).
121	
122	The parser (Lua: detects `...`, bash: detects `$@` after shift) can auto-populate
123	`variadic_inputs` when a file is selected via the file browser. For other languages
124	(C, etc.) the user sets the toggle manually in the inspector — the executor contract
125	already supports N positional args via `arg_count`, so C functions reading `argv[]`
126	in a loop work without any changes.
127	
128	#### Auto-grow
129	When a wire is connected to the LAST slot of a variadic input, the editor appends
130	a new empty slot (`text_N+1`) to `box.inputs` and saves the box. The new slot
131	appears immediately as an open port.
132	
133	#### Auto-shrink
134	When a wire is disconnected from any slot of a variadic input:
135	1. Remove that slot from `box.inputs`.
136	2. Rename all subsequent slots down by one (`text_2` → `text_1`, etc.).
137	3. Update every connection that referenced a renamed slot:
138	   - `to_input` in the connection record on this box
139	   - The corresponding side on the source box (connections are stored in both
140	     endpoint files; both must be updated atomically).
141	4. Keep at least one slot for the variadic input (never shrink below `text_0`).
142	
143	Middle-port removal is handled by the same rename-and-compact step: remove the cut
144	slot, shift all higher-indexed slots down, update their wires. The user always sees
145	a dense, gap-free list of ports.
146	
147	## Open questions before implementing Part B
148	- Rename atomicity: the server's `PUT /maps/:name/boxes/:id` writes one box at a
149	  time. Updating the connection in the source box requires a second PUT. These are
150	  two separate writes — consider whether a partial failure leaves the map in an
151	  inconsistent state, and whether a `PUT /maps/:name/connections` batch endpoint
152	  is worth adding first.
153	- Should the file browser parser set `variadic_inputs` automatically when it
154	  detects `...` in a Lua signature, and if so, which argument specifically?
155	
156	  -- nah let's just make the user set the variadic property manually. Unless we can put it in the language driver
157	     somehow? but I don't think that's really possible in the design. Thoughts?
158	
159	## Suggested implementation sequence
160	1. Part A: implement `libs/text.lua` with `M.concat(sep, ...)`.
161	2. Part B schema: add `variadic_inputs` to box schema and validator.
162	3. Part B editor: auto-grow on wire connect; auto-shrink + rename on wire disconnect.
163	4. Part B inspector: variadic toggle per input port.
164	5. Part B parser: auto-detect `...` in Lua and set `variadic_inputs`.
165	
166	## Future development ideas
167	
168	### Language-spec-driven variadic detection
169	
170	Currently the editor uses hard-coded heuristics per language to find
171	variadic indicators in a function signature (Lua: literal `...`, Bash:
172	`$@` after `shift`). The cleaner architecture mirrors the phase 3
173	language-spec model (issue 303): each language declares its own way
174	to recognize and surface variadic parameters, and the file-browser
175	parser asks the language spec rather than embedding the rules.
176	
177	Concretely, the editor side of a language spec
178	(`langs/<name>/lexer.js` or a sibling `parser.js`) could expose:
179	
180	```js
181	// Returns { name, inputs: ["sep", "text"], variadic_inputs: ["text"] }
182	// for `function M.concat(sep, ...)` in Lua, or
183	// `concat() { local sep="$1"; shift; ... "$@" }` in Bash.
184	function parse_function_signature(source, fn_name) { ... }
185	```
186	
187	The editor still calls the same on-select handler; the language spec
188	is responsible for deciding how to translate native variadic syntax
189	into the editor's `(inputs, variadic_inputs)` model.
190	
191	### Variadic shapes that don't map cleanly to an array
192	
193	The current model assumes variadic = an ordered series of positional
194	slots. That fits Lua `...`, C `va_list`, Bash `"$@"`, JavaScript rest
195	params, etc. It does not fit:
196	
197	- **Keyword arguments** (Python `**kwargs`, Ruby keyword splats) —
198	  these are key/value pairs, not positional. A box would need
199	  named-slot pairs the user labels per slot, not auto-numbered
200	  `_0` / `_1` / `_2`.
201	- **Tagged unions / discriminated lists** (Rust enum lists, Haskell
202	  variadic via heterogeneous lists) — each entry has a type tag in
203	  addition to a value. Two-field slots, or per-slot type metadata.
204	- **Streaming inputs** (generators, channels, iterators) — the
205	  consumer doesn't know in advance how many values arrive. This is
206	  closer to issue 213's queued-input model than to variadic.
207	
208	A future revision of the editor's variadic UI could let language
209	specs choose between several "variadic shapes":
210	
211	| shape           | slot model                          | example use      |
212	|-----------------|-------------------------------------|------------------|
213	| `positional`    | `<base>_<index>` ordered (current)  | Lua `...`        |
214	| `keyed`         | per-slot `(key, value)` pairs       | Python `**kwargs`|
215	| `tagged`        | per-slot `(tag, value)` pairs       | discriminated unions |
216	| `stream`        | one slot, queued (issue 213)        | iterators        |
217	
218	Each shape would have its own auto-grow rule and inspector controls.
219	The language spec declares which shape applies to a given variadic
220	parameter, and the editor renders accordingly.
221	
222	This is speculative — the positional shape covers every shipped
223	language so far. Capturing it here so the design door stays open.
224	
225	## Relevant files
226	- `libs/ollama.lua` — reference pattern for a library box
227	- `assets/js/006-wires.js` — wire connect/disconnect events
228	- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — input port list, toggle rendering
229	- `src/001-schema.lua` — box validator (`variadic_inputs` field)
230	- `src/005-http-server.lua` — PUT box endpoint (rename propagation)
231	- `issues/208-port-literal-values.md` — `sep` will likely use literal port values
232	
```

{
  "content":"# 234 — Middle-click duplicates the selected box (with wires)\n\n## Status\nopen\n\n## Current behavior\n\nA box on the canvas can be selected (single click) and edited via\nthe inspector, but there is no shortcut for \"make me another one\njust like this.\" A user who wants to repeat a configured box has\nto:\n\n1. Right-click → \"add box here\" to spawn a fresh empty box.\n2. Reopen the file browser, re-pick the same ref / fn.\n3. Re-type all the literal input values.\n4. Re-wire every upstream connection by hand.\n\nFor a box with several literal values and several incoming wires\nthis is many clicks of pure repetition, and any one of them is a\nchance to typo a value or miss a wire.\n\nMiddle-mouse currently has no editor behavior other than the\nbrowser's default scroll-anchor (which is itself disruptive on a\ncanvas app).\n\n## Intended behavior\n\n**When a box is selected and the user does a short middle-mouse\nclick (not a drag) anywhere on the canvas**, the editor creates a\nduplicate of the selected box at the cursor position. The duplicate\ncarries over:\n\n- `ref`, `fn`, `kind`, `outputs`, `variadic_inputs` — same function\n  bound to the new box.\n- Every input port, including its literal value (if any).\n- Every wire whose destination is the original box: a matching wire\n  is created from the same source box / output to the new box's\n  same-named input. Outgoing wires (where the original is the\n  source) are NOT duplicated — multiple boxes fanning out from one\n  source is the normal case; multiple boxes converging on the same\n  downstream port is not.\n\nThe duplicate gets a fresh `id` and a `ui.x / ui.y` at the cursor\nlocation. The label is the original's label suffixed `(copy)` (or\n`(copy 2)`, `(copy 3)` if the suffix is already present) — same\nnaming pattern most desktop UIs use.\n\nA middle-drag (the cursor moved more than a few pixels between\npress and release) is not a duplicate. Reserve that gesture for\nfuture pan / other use; for now it is a no-op rather than an\naccidental copy.\n\nIf no box is selected, middle-click is a no-op (with maybe a\ntoast / status hint, \"select a box to duplicate it\").\n\n### Why middle-click\n\nRight-click already opens the context menu (issue 212). Left-click\nselects / deselects. Middle-click is the only unused primary\nbutton, and the \"click on empty canvas to place\" muscle memory from\nadd-box mode transfers cleanly: select source → middle-click target.\n\n### Why duplicate incoming wires by default\n\nThe whole reason to duplicate is \"I want another node fed by the\nsame data.\" Forcing the user to re-wire defeats the gesture.\nOutgoing wires are skipped because the user usually wants the copy\nto feed something *new* downstream — that's the half they're about\nto wire up.\n\n### Open question — duplicate-with-no-wires variant?\n\nHold a modifier (`Shift+middle-click`?) to duplicate without\nwires? Defer until someone asks; the wireless version is just \"add\na fresh box and browse to the same fn,\" which the right-click menu\nalready covers.\n\n## Suggested implementation steps\n\n1. `assets/js/005-app.js` — middle-mouse handlers:\n   - `mousedown` (button 1): record press position + selected box id.\n   - `mouseup` (button 1): if movement ≤ threshold and a box is\n     selected, call `duplicate_box(selected_id, cursor_world_pos)`.\n   - `preventDefault` on the mousedown to suppress browser\n     scroll-anchor behavior.\n2. New module function `Boxes.duplicate(src_box, x, y)`:\n   - Clone the box JSON, assign a new id, set `ui.x/ui.y`.\n   - Suffix the label with `(copy)` / `(copy N)`.\n   - POST to the server (existing create-box endpoint).\n3. Wire duplication: iterate every connection in the map whose\n   `to_box == src_box.id`. For each, POST a matching connection\n   with `to_box = new_box.id` (same `to_input`, same\n   `from_box` / `from_output`).\n4. Update local Boxes cache and re-render. Select the new box so\n   the user can immediately edit / nudge it.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `assets/js/005-app.js` — pointer event handlers, current\n  right-click context menu lives here.\n- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — local box cache + create flow.\n- `assets/js/006-wires.js` — `create_connection`, wire data model.\n- `src/005-http-server.lua` — existing POST endpoints for boxes\n  and connections.\n- `issues/completed/212-editor-interaction-modes.md` — established\n  the right-click and tool-mode patterns this issue extends.\n",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/234-middle-click-duplicate-box.md"

{
  "content":"# 235 — Literal value replaces port name on the canvas (when set)\n\n## Status\nopen\n\n## Current behavior\n\nEach input port renders as a dot on the left edge of a box, with\nthe port's name (e.g. `text`, `sep`, `prompt`) drawn next to it on\nthe canvas. The literal value of that port — when one has been\ntyped into the inspector (issue 208 / 224) — does not appear on the\ncanvas at all. To see what value is bound, the user opens the\ninspector and reads the value field.\n\nFor a box like `concat(sep, text_0, text_1)` where `sep` is a\nliteral `\"\\n\\n\"`, the canvas shows the parameter name `sep` but\nnot the actual separator. A user skimming the map sees the\nstructure but not the content.\n\n## Intended behavior\n\nIf a port has a literal value set in the inspector, **the canvas\nlabel for that port shows the value instead of the port's name**.\nThe inspector still shows the original port name in its name field\n(unchanged) and the value in its value field (unchanged) — only the\ncanvas rendering changes.\n\nExamples:\n\n| port name | literal value     | canvas label |\n|-----------|-------------------|--------------|\n| `sep`     | `\"\\n\\n\"`          | `\"\\n\\n\"`     |\n| `prompt`  | (none)            | `prompt`     |\n| `path`    | `\"./input.txt\"`   | `\"./input.txt\"` |\n| `count`   | `5`               | `5`          |\n\nThe value is the same string the user typed; quotes are part of\nhow the user wrote it. Long values get truncated with an ellipsis\nat a reasonable width (~24 chars?) and the full value shows in a\ntooltip on hover.\n\n### Why\n\nThis is the same idea as a spreadsheet cell showing the rendered\nvalue while the formula bar shows the formula. A user wants to see\n*what the program does* — the configured value — without having to\ninspect each box one at a time. The port name is meta-information\n(useful when wiring, useful in the inspector); the value is the\ninformation (useful when reading the map).\n\n### Wires override values\n\nIf a wire is connected to a port, the wire is the source of truth\nand any literal value is ignored at runtime. The canvas label in\nthat case shows the **port name**, not the value — the value isn't\nwhat's flowing through, and showing it would mislead. Three states:\n\n- No wire, no value → show port name.\n- No wire, literal value set → show the value.\n- Wire connected → show the port name (value, if any, is dormant).\n\n### Edge cases\n\n- **Multi-line value** (e.g. a prompt with `\\n` in it): render the\n  first line only on the canvas, ellipsis if there's more, full\n  value in the tooltip.\n- **Empty string value** (`\"\"`): treat as \"no value\" — show the\n  port name. Otherwise the user types accidentally and the port\n  silently disappears from view.\n- **Variadic slots** (`text_0`, `text_1`, …): the same rule\n  applies per slot. A variadic group with each slot's value shown\n  is one of the strongest cases for this feature.\n\n## Suggested implementation steps\n\n1. `assets/js/002-boxes.js::draw_box` — the loop that draws\n   input port labels. Replace the unconditional `port.name` with:\n   ```\n   const wire_attached = wire_targets_port(box, port);\n   const has_value = port.value != null && port.value !== \"\";\n   const label = (!wire_attached && has_value) ? truncate(port.value) : port.name;\n   ```\n2. Hover / tooltip: if the canvas label is a truncated value,\n   register a small hit-region so the existing tooltip mechanism\n   (or a fresh one) can show the full value on mouseover.\n3. Re-render on inspector value edit: the existing autosave path\n   already invalidates the canvas; verify it still triggers a\n   redraw after this change.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — port label rendering\n- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — value field that drives the data\n- `assets/js/006-wires.js` — `wire_targets_port` helper (or write\n  one if not present)\n- `issues/completed/208-port-literal-values.md` — established\n  literal values; this issue surfaces them visually\n- `issues/completed/224-editable-port-names-and-canvas-value-edit.md`\n  — explicitly deferred canvas-value-edit; this is a related but\n  smaller display-only change, not the editing UI from 224\n",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/235-literal-value-replaces-port-name-on-canvas.md"
}
```

{
  "content":"# 236 — Box header shows filename; output label shows `fn_name()` call form\n\n## Status\nopen\n\n## Current behavior\n\nA call box's header (the bold text at the top of the box on the\ncanvas) currently displays the box's label — by default, the\nfunction name (e.g. `concat`, `read_text`, `prompt_ollama`). The\noutput port on the right edge has a generic label (`output` /\n`result` / whatever the parser produced).\n\nFor a user reading a map, the header gives one of the two pieces\nof relevant identity (the function), but the other piece — *which\nfile the function comes from* — is missing from the visible box.\nYou can see `concat` and not know whether it is `libs/text.lua`'s\nconcat or some other module's concat without opening the inspector\nand reading `ref`.\n\nThe single output label, `output`, is generic and conveys nothing\nabout what's being computed.\n\n## Intended behavior\n\nReinterpret the two text slots on a box:\n\n- **Box header (top)** = the **filename** of `ref` (basename, no\n  path prefix, e.g. `text.lua`). This is the module / file the\n  function lives in.\n- **Output port label (right edge)** = the function call form,\n  `fn_name()`, e.g. `concat()`, `read_text()`, `prompt_ollama()`.\n  This reads as \"this wire carries the return value of calling\n  fn_name.\"\n\nSo a box that calls `concat` from `libs/text.lua` reads, top to\nbottom on the canvas:\n\n```\n┌─ text.lua ────────────────┐\n│ ● sep      \"\\n\\n\"          │\n│ ● text_0                   │   concat() ●\n│ ● text_1                   │\n└────────────────────────────┘\n```\n\n(Combined with issue 235, the `sep` literal also shows on the\ncanvas — included here for illustration of how the canvas reads as\na whole.)\n\n### Why this split\n\nA reader scanning a map mentally parses it as a series of function\ncalls. The function call form `fn_name()` is the natural way to\nread \"this is the value of calling fn_name with these inputs.\" The\nfilename header sits one layer up: \"this function comes from this\nmodule.\" Together they read like an annotated call site — module\non top, return value on the side.\n\nIt also frees the box header from having to be unique. Right now\nthe default label is the function name, which collides across\nboxes that all call the same function. The filename groups by\nmodule — many boxes can share `text.lua` as their header and that\nis fine; what distinguishes them is what they compute and how\nthey are wired.\n\n### Label override\n\nThe user-editable `box.label` field remains. If a user sets an\nexplicit label, it overrides the filename in the header. Use case:\nmultiple boxes in the same map call `text.concat`, and the user\nwants to name them by their role (\"join paragraphs\", \"join CSV\nrow\") rather than by module. The override is intentional and only\napplied when set; default behavior is filename-from-ref.\n\nThe output port label is not user-editable in this issue — it is\nalways derived from `fn`. (If a use case appears, add it later.)\n\n### Filename derivation\n\n`ref` is a path like `libs/text.lua` or `maps/foo/src/echo.sh`.\nTake the basename (`text.lua`, `echo.sh`). Do not strip the\nextension — the extension says which language driver runs, which\nis information the user cares about.\n\nFor boxes without a `ref` (branch boxes, comparators, future\ndispatch-layer primitives like the data source from issue 229),\nfall back to the kind name (`branch`, `data`, etc.) in the header\nslot, and skip the `fn()` output label (or show the primitive's\nown conventional output name).\n\n## Suggested implementation steps\n\n1. `assets/js/002-boxes.js::draw_box` — header text path:\n   - If `box.label` is set and non-empty, use it (current behavior).\n   - Else, derive from `box.ref`: basename of the path string.\n   - Else (no ref), use `box.kind` as a final fallback.\n2. Output port label path: when `box.fn` is set, render\n   `${box.fn}()` instead of `output` / `result`. Otherwise leave\n   the existing default.\n3. `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — the inspector's \"label\" field\n   shows the override value (the explicit user-set label) and its\n   placeholder shows what the canvas is using by default. So the\n   user sees \"currently displayed: text.lua (from ref)\" and can\n   override.\n\n## Open questions\n\n- **Path vs basename for the header**: a deeply-nested ref like\n  `maps/foo/src/sub/echo.sh` becomes just `echo.sh` in the header.\n  Is the parent dir worth keeping (`sub/echo.sh`) for disambiguation?\n  Probably not by default — the inspector shows the full ref —\n  but worth revisiting if collisions cause confusion.\n- **Branch / data / future primitives**: their headers and output\n  labels need conventions of their own. Capture per-kind defaults\n  in one place (a small kind-to-header map) so future kinds add\n  one entry.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — box draw routine\n- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — label field + placeholder hint\n- `issues/completed/207-source-file-browser-and-port-auto-population.md`\n  — ref/fn pairing established here\n- `issues/completed/210-llm-route-box.md` — single-output redesign\n  this issue's `fn()` label leans on\n- `issues/235-literal-value-replaces-port-name-on-canvas.md` — sister\n  display change for input ports\n",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/236-box-header-filename-output-shows-call-form.md"
}

{
  "content":"# 237 — Expand the bundled string-manipulation library\n\n## Status\nopen\n\n## Current behavior\n\n`libs/text.lua` (created in issue 217) exposes two functions:\n\n- `M.concat(sep, ...)` — variadic join with a separator\n- `M.split(text, sep)` — split a string on a plain separator,\n  returns a JSON-encoded array\n\nThat covers two of the most common string operations and was\ndeliberately minimal — just enough for the demo map that drove 217.\nA user wanting to upper-case, lower-case, find-and-replace, trim,\nor substring has to either write the function themselves into a\nproject-local file, or fall back to a Lua call box pointing at\n`string.upper` / `string.gsub` directly (which works but is more\ntyping per box).\n\nThere is also no canonical home for \"the string library that ships\nwith the editor.\" `libs/text.lua` exists but its scope was set by\none issue's needs, not by a broader plan.\n\n## Intended behavior\n\n`libs/text.lua` grows into a small, opinionated standard library\nof string operations, each wired to be variadic-friendly and\nsingle-output where it makes sense. The full set in this issue:\n\n| function                          | inputs                          | output                          |\n|-----------------------------------|----------------------------------|---------------------------------|\n| `M.concat(sep, ...)`              | sep, variadic text               | joined string (already exists)  |\n| `M.split(text, sep)`              | text, sep                        | JSON array of parts (exists)    |\n| `M.upper(text)`                   | text                             | upper-cased string              |\n| `M.lower(text)`                   | text                             | lower-cased string              |\n| `M.trim(text)`                    | text                             | leading + trailing ws stripped  |\n| `M.replace(text, find, repl)`     | text, find, repl                 | every occurrence replaced       |\n| `M.replace_first(text, find, repl)`| text, find, repl                | first occurrence only           |\n| `M.contains(text, needle)`        | text, needle                     | boolean (as `\"true\"`/`\"false\"`) |\n| `M.starts_with(text, prefix)`     | text, prefix                     | boolean                         |\n| `M.ends_with(text, suffix)`       | text, suffix                     | boolean                         |\n| `M.length(text)`                  | text                             | integer (as string on wire)     |\n| `M.substring(text, start, len)`   | text, start, len (1-indexed)     | substring                       |\n\nAll `find` / `prefix` / `suffix` arguments are **plain strings**,\nnot Lua patterns — same convention as `M.split`. A user who wants\npatterns can call `string.gsub` directly via a call box. The\nlibrary's job is \"common case, no surprises.\"\n\n`M.replace_first` is a separate function rather than a flag on\n`replace` because the editor has no boolean-port primitive yet\n(literal values are strings; \"all or first\" toggles cleanly\nbetween two function names). Could collapse later when a primitive\nboolean exists.\n\n### Companion info.md\n\nUpdate `libs/text.lua.info.md` so each function gets its own entry\nwith inputs / outputs / one-line description. Issue 207's file\nbrowser parses signatures from source; the info.md is the\nhuman-skim version. (Per CLAUDE.md: prefer reading info.md over\nsource for an overview.)\n\n### Why these specifically\n\nSkim a handful of maps and the same operations show up: cleaning\ninput (trim), normalizing case (upper / lower), simple\nsubstitution (replace), boolean guards (contains / starts_with /\nends_with), array slicing (substring). The list above covers the\n\"would you have written this as a helper anyway?\" cases without\nsprawling into a kitchen-sink std lib.\n\nOperations explicitly **not** in this issue:\n\n- Regex / pattern matching — a separate `libs/regex.lua` issue.\n  Pattern syntax is its own UI problem (which dialect? escape\n  rules?) and deserves a dedicated home.\n- Encode / decode (base64, URL, hex) — separate `libs/codec.lua`.\n- Format strings (`printf`-style) — single-output-wire constraint\n  makes the call shape awkward; revisit when the language spec for\n  format string handling firms up.\n\n### Bundled location convention\n\nFiles in `libs/` are the bundled, edit-as-default modules every\nmap can `ref` directly without per-map setup. This issue does not\nchange that — it just fills out one of those bundled modules.\n\nA future per-language convention (issue 303 territory) might split\n`libs/` by language (`libs/lua/text.lua`, `libs/c/text.c`,\n`libs/bash/text.sh`). Today everything in `libs/` is Lua; revisit\nthe split when a second language ships a bundled standard library.\n\n## Suggested implementation steps\n\n1. `libs/text.lua` — append the new functions. Each is a 1-3 line\n   wrapper over a `string.*` builtin or a small primitive. Keep\n   them order-aligned with the table above for readability.\n2. `libs/text.lua.info.md` — add one entry per new function\n   following the existing format. Include the \"plain string, not\n   pattern\" note where applicable.\n3. A small test map (`tests/maps/text-lib/`?) — one box per\n   function, hand-traced expected outputs. The phase-2 integration\n   tests can pick it up automatically (issue 311 wiring).\n4. The demo for the current phase should pick up at least one or\n   two of these on its display path so the user sees the library\n   in action.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `libs/text.lua` — host of the new functions\n- `libs/text.lua.info.md` — documentation companion\n- `issues/completed/217-concat-box-and-dynamic-inputs.md` — founded\n  this library with concat + split\n- `issues/238-editor-http-server-config-file.md` — paired issue\n  that makes \"where do bundled libs live\" configurable, so a user\n  can add their own bundled-default directory alongside `libs/`\n",

{
  "content":"# 238 — Editor HTTP server config file (default bundled-script locations)\n\n## Status\nopen\n\n## Current behavior\n\nThe editor's HTTP server (`src/005-http-server.lua`, issue 105) is\nlaunched with map-relative paths discovered at startup time. The\nfile browser (issue 211, 211a) sees the map's own `src/` directory\nplus the unified pipeline dirs the runner sets up, but the set of\n\"bundled / default\" library locations the editor offers is\nhard-coded in the server / file-browser logic.\n\nIf a user wants to drop in their own personal library of bundled\nscripts (e.g. their own `text-extras.lua`, their own\n`my-team-helpers/` directory) and have the editor's file browser\ntreat them as first-class default refs, there is no per-deployment\nway to point the server at additional directories. They would have\nto edit the server source.\n\nThere is also no record of the editor's runtime configuration —\nports, default map dir, asset dir, the bundled lib dirs — in a\nconfig file the user can read or version. The defaults live in\ncode.\n\n## Intended behavior\n\nIntroduce a config file the editor's HTTP server reads at startup\nto learn:\n\n- **Server port** (default `7474` or whichever current default).\n- **Map root directory** (default `maps/`).\n- **Bundled script locations** — a list of directory paths the\n  file browser exposes as default-available refs, distinct from\n  the current map's own `src/`. Default list includes `libs/`.\n- **Asset directory** for the editor (default `assets/`).\n- **Log / tmp directory** if relevant (per the project's tmp\n  symlink convention).\n\nEach entry has a sensible default; missing entries fall back to\nthose defaults. The user can override any subset by editing the\nfile.\n\n### File location and format\n\nPath: `config/editor-server.lua` (returns a Lua table) or\n`config/editor-server.json`. Lean toward the Lua table — every\nother config in this project is Lua, and a Lua return-table lets\nthe user compute paths if needed (e.g. `os.getenv(\"HOME\") .. \"/my-libs\"`).\n\nShape:\n\n```lua\nreturn {\n  port = 7474,\n  map_root = \"maps\",\n  asset_dir = \"assets\",\n  bundled_script_dirs = {\n    \"libs\",           -- the in-tree default\n    -- \"~/.soramech/my-libs\",  -- example user override\n  },\n  tmp_dir = \"tmp\",\n}\n```\n\nA startup helper in `src/006-server-main.lua` loads the config,\nfills in defaults, and passes the resolved table down to the HTTP\nserver module. The server prints the resolved config on boot so\nthe user can confirm what's in effect.\n\n### File browser surfacing\n\nThe file browser (issue 211 + 211a) currently lists the map's own\n`src/` and the pipeline-resolved bundled dirs. With this config in\nplace, the bundled list comes from `bundled_script_dirs` directly.\nA second pane (or expandable section per dir) labels each entry by\nits source directory so the user can tell their own bundle apart\nfrom the in-tree `libs/`.\n\n### Why a file, not CLI flags\n\nTwo reasons:\n1. Reproducibility — the config file checks into a personal dotfile\n   or repo, and the editor starts up the same way every time.\n2. Discoverability — a user reading their `config/` directory sees\n   what's customizable. CLI flags are invisible until the user runs\n   `--help`.\n\nCLI flags can layer on top if needed (a `--config-file` override),\nbut the file is the primary mechanism.\n\n### Defaults vs explicit\n\nThe server still works with no config file present — every field\nhas a default and the file is optional. Creating the file is a\n\"customize me\" gesture, not a setup requirement.\n\n## Open questions\n\n- **Per-map config overrides**: a map could ship its own\n  `config/editor-server.lua` that adds its own bundled dirs. Useful?\n  Probably yes long-term, but defer until a real use case appears.\n  The global config is enough for now.\n- **Path resolution**: bundled dirs as absolute paths, project-\n  relative, or both? Both. If the path starts with `/` or `~`,\n  treat as absolute (after `~` expansion); otherwise resolve\n  against the project root. Document this in a comment at the top\n  of the config file.\n- **Hot reload**: changes to the config file requiring a server\n  restart vs picked up on next request? Restart is simpler and\n  matches the existing model. Hot reload is a separate issue if\n  ever wanted.\n\n## Suggested implementation steps\n\n1. New file `config/editor-server.lua` (or a documented sample\n   under `config/editor-server.example.lua`) with the schema above\n   and the defaults filled in.\n2. `src/006-server-main.lua` — `load_server_config(path)`:\n   - `pcall(dofile, path)` — fall back to empty table if missing.\n   - Merge over a defaults table.\n   - `~` expansion on string paths.\n   - Print resolved config on stdout at boot.\n3. `src/005-http-server.lua` — accept the resolved config table as\n   its constructor argument. Use `bundled_script_dirs` in the\n   `handle_list_src` family so the file browser sees them.\n4. `assets/js/007-filebrowser.js` — render bundled dirs as a\n   labeled second pane, separate from the current map's `src/`.\n5. ts: 2026-05-19T03:03:31.459Z

Five issues created: 234 (middle-click duplicate), 235 (value replaces port name), 236 (filename header + `fn()` output), 237 (text library expansion), 238 (server config file).

Now — the data box one. You asked for help managing it, so let me lay out what I see and ask back:

**What you already have**: issue 229 (open) proposes splitting data IO into **two boxes** — a `data` source (path → emits file bytes) and a paired `file_write` sink (path + text inputs → writes to disk). It is language-agnostic and lives in the dispatch layer.

**What you described today** sounds like a *third* design: **one box** that is both a read and a write, dispatching on input type — pointer → memory location in the thread pool slot store, string → filepath. Plus a UI slider for the "no input given" case to choose between `/tmp/`-text-file and C-memory-pointer-from-slot-store.

I think you got tangled because three different axes are mixing in one box:

1. **read vs write** — is this thing a source or a sink?
2. **disk vs RAM (slot store)** — where does the data live?
3. **input-driven path vs static path / auto-allocated** — does the user wire in the location, type a literal, or let the box pick?

229 cleanly splits axis 1 (two boxes). Your idea today collapses all three into one box and uses input *type* (pointer vs string) as the disambiguator. That's elegant in one way (one node, fewer concepts) and tangled in another (the box's behavior changes shape based on what is wired in, which is the kind of thing that bites later).

A couple of questions back to you before I write anything:

- **Why a pointer at all in the editor surface?** The slot store (issue 302) already gives every wire a slot ID — that *is* the "C memory location." If a box wants to talk about RAM, the slot is the natural handle, not a raw pointer. Would "save to slot N / read from slot N" feel right, where N is just an integer wire?
- **Is the "/tmp/ vs C memory" slider the same axis as "is the input a path string or a pointer"?** If yes, the slider is redundant when something is wired (the input type already says which). If no, then there are really two switches and the slider is doing something separate — what?
- **Do you want one box that flips modes, or two boxes that each do one thing well?** 229 already chose the second path. Is today's idea a revision of 229, or a different thing that should live alongside it?

If you want, I can either (a) **update 229** to absorb your new thinking (probably re-scope it as "language-agnostic IO primitives — source and sink, with disk and RAM-slot variants"), or (b) **leave 229 alone and write a fresh issue** for the unified read/write data box if that turns out to be a separate concept. Per the project's "build history vertically" rule, I lean toward updating 229 — but I'd rather you tell me which of those three axes you actually care about before I commit either way.

It carries a `ref` and an\n`fn` like a call box and runs through whichever language driver\nmatches the file extension. The only difference is the green badge\non the canvas — pure cosmetic.\n\n`libs/files.lua::read_text` (issue 216, complete) covers actual\n\"read a text file\" via a regular call box, but only from Lua. A\nBash function that needs file contents has to wire through a Lua\nread box, which is a language-coupling no graph reader expects.\n\n## Intended behavior — three kinds: `call`, `read`, `write`\n\nThree primitive box kinds. `call` is unchanged. `read` and `write`\nare new, dispatch-layer primitives — no language driver runs.\n\n### `read` — file path in, contents out\n\nA `read` box has one input port and one output port:\n\n| port | direction | type   | meaning                            |\n|------|-----------|--------|-------------------------------------|\n| path | in        | string | path to the file on disk            |\n| (single output) | out | string | the file's contents, UTF-8 |\n\nNo `ref`, no `fn`. The dispatch layer reads the file when the box\nfires and pushes the contents onto the output wire.\n\nStatic path option: `read` boxes may also carry a `path` field in\ntheir JSON for the common \"read this fixed file\" case. If the\n`path` input wire is connected, the wire wins; otherwise the JSON\nfield provides the path. (Same override rule wires already follow\nfor literal port values everywhere else.)\n\n### `write` — file path + value in, completion signal out\n\nA `write` box has two input ports and one optional output port:\n\n| port  | direction | type   | meaning                            |\n|-------|-----------|--------|-------------------------------------|\n| path  | in        | string | destination path                    |\n| value | in        | any    | the content to write (see below)    |\n| (single output) | out | boolean | `\"true\"` when the write completed |\n\nThe `value` input is \"any kind\" — whatever comes through the wire\ngets serialized to a UTF-8 string and written to disk. Files are bytes;\nevery language emits bytes; the read-and-emit / receive-and-write\nsteps have no language-specific anything. Pulling them into the\ndispatch layer makes them available identically from every\nlanguage.\n\nIt also keeps the graph readable: a user sees\n`config.json → parse → ...` and `... → save → result.json` and\nimmediately knows where the data flows in from and out to. The\ncurrent alternative — call boxes pointing at language-specific\nread / write functions — works but buries the IO behind a function\nreference.\n\n### Atomic writes\n\n`write` writes to `path.tmp`, fsyncs, then renames to `path`. This\nmatches how `soramech-data` already handles its writes. Crash-safe\nby default; no flag to disable. If someone needs append semantics\nor non-atomic writes later, that is a different kind (e.g.\n`append`) rather than a flag on this one.\n\n### Binary data — out of scope\n\nUTF-8 encoding of arbitrary bytes can fail. Both `read` and `write`\ntreat their contents as UTF-8 strings; non-UTF-8 input on `read`\nsurfaces a clear error rather than silently mojibake-ing. Binary\ndata revisits when a real use case appears (a separate kind, or a\nflag, or a base64 convention — pick when needed).\n\n### JSON parsing stays downstream\n\n`read` emits raw text. ts: 2026-05-19T03:11:40.170Z

Sure, let me back up.

Issue 236 says **the box header should display the filename of the function the box calls** (its `ref`). E.g. a box pointing at `libs/text.lua::concat` shows `text.lua` in the header.

That rule works fine for `call` boxes — they have a `ref` pointing at a source file. But the editor has other kinds of boxes too:

- `read` and `write` (new, from updated 229) — no ref, they're dispatch-layer primitives
- `branch` — predicate routing, no ref
- `iterator` (issue 221) — loops, no ref
- `comparator` (the lt/eq/gt output mode) — also has no ref of its own

For those, there's no filename to put in the header. So *something else* has to go there by default.

In 236 I left a fallback rule: "if there's no ref, use the kind name." That means a `read` box's header would just say `read`, a `branch` would say `branch`, etc. Functional, but kind of bland — and somebody has to decide whether `read`'s header should say `"read"` or `"read file"` or just an icon, etc.

**That decision — what each kind's default header text is — needs to live somewhere.** It's a one-line-per-kind lookup table:

| kind | default header |
|------|---------------|
| call | (basename of ref) |
| read | "read" |
| write | "write" |
| branch | "branch" |
| iterator | "iter" |
| comparator | "?" |

My question was: do you want me to **pick those default strings now** and write them into 236 (or into a new tiny issue), or **leave it open** for whoever implements 236 to pick on the fly?

Either is fine — I just wanted to flag that the table doesn't exist yet and somebody will have to fill it in.

75	
76	The output port label is not user-editable in this issue — it is
77	always derived from `fn`. (If a use case appears, add it later.)
78	
79	### Filename derivation
80	
81	`ref` is a path like `libs/text.lua` or `maps/foo/src/echo.sh`.
82	Take the basename (`text.lua`, `echo.sh`). Do not strip the
83	extension — the extension says which language driver runs, which
84	is information the user cares about.
85	
86	For boxes without a `ref` (branch boxes, comparators, future
87	dispatch-layer primitives like the data source from issue 229),
88	fall back to the kind name (`branch`, `data`, etc.) in the header
89	slot, and skip the `fn()` output label (or show the primitive's
90	own conventional output name).
91	
92	## Suggested implementation steps
93	
94	1. `assets/js/002-boxes.js::draw_box` — header text path:
95	   - If `box.label` is set and non-empty, use it (current behavior).
96	   - Else, derive from `box.ref`: basename of the path string.
97	   - Else (no ref), use `box.kind` as a final fallback.
98	2. Output port label path: when `box.fn` is set, render
99	   `${box.fn}()` instead of `output` / `result`. Otherwise leave
100	   the existing default.
101	3. `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — the inspector's "label" field
102	   shows the override value (the explicit user-set label) and its
103	   placeholder shows what the canvas is using by default. So the
104	   user sees "currently displayed: text.lua (from ref)" and can
105	   override.
106	
107	## Open questions
108	
109	- **Path vs basename for the header**: a deeply-nested ref like
110	  `maps/foo/src/sub/echo.sh` becomes just `echo.sh` in the header.
111	  Is the parent dir worth keeping (`sub/echo.sh`) for disambiguation?
112	  Probably not by default — the inspector shows the full ref —
113	  but worth revisiting if collisions cause confusion.
114	- **Branch / data / future primitives**: their headers and output
115	  labels need conventions of their own. Capture per-kind defaults

117	  one entry.
