### [893] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:14:12.226Z

Here's where we stand:

**Code today**: `data` is recognized by the schema as a valid `kind` (alongside `call`) and offered in the inspector's kind dropdown, but it has **no distinct runtime semantics** — the executor treats it like a `call` box. The only difference is the green badge color on the canvas. Functionally cosmetic.

**Issue 229 (open)**: I redesigned the spec earlier to replace `data` with two dispatch-layer primitives — `read` (path → contents) and `write` (path + value → done signal). Neither is implemented yet. The design hasn't included a "literal output value" mode.

**What you're proposing** is a third mode I hadn't captured in 229: the data box itself can carry an inline literal output value. Logic:

- If a literal `output_value` is set in the inspector → emit that directly, no I/O.
- Else if a `path` input is supplied (wire or literal) → read the file at path and emit its contents.
- Else → error.

This is the same precedence rule input ports already follow (wire > literal value), just applied to the output side. Same mental model.

This is a nicer design than 229's current "two separate kinds" approach in one important way: a user who just wants to drop a small inline config value into a graph (a port, a prompt, a constant) doesn't need a real file at all — they type it into the inspector and that's the box. If they later want to externalize it, they can swap the literal for a `path` input pointing at a file.

**Decision point**: do you want me to **redesign 229** around this single-`data`-kind-with-literal-or-file behavior (keeping `write` as a separate sink kind, since it really is a different concern), or **leave 229 as-is and open a new issue** for "literal output on data boxes" as a smaller, separately-implementable feature on top?

My recommendation: update 229 in place. The literal-output idea subsumes the `read` half cleanly, and you've already once-redesigned 229 in this conversation — the design history thread stays on one ticket.

### [897] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:17:53.814Z

### [898] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:18:09.345Z

You can combine all three — the question is whether the cost is worth it. Here's where the edges are:

**1. Two ways to source the output → which wins?**
If a box has both a literal output value AND a file path configured, the output port has two possible sources. You'd need a precedence rule (literal beats file, or vice versa) and the user has to remember it. The input-port rule today is "wire beats literal" — extending that to outputs is consistent but not obvious at a glance.

**2. Read and write share the same `path`.**
If the box has a `path` configured AND a `write_value` wire AND no output literal, is it reading or writing? You'd pick by presence of `write_value` — wire connected → write mode, no wire → read mode. Workable, but a user who *forgets* to wire the `write_value` ends up with a silent read of the destination file they meant to write to. That's a real footgun.

**3. The output port means different things in each mode.**
- Read mode → emits file contents (possibly large text).
- Write mode → emits `true` / done.
- Literal mode → emits whatever the user typed.

Downstream boxes wired to that port can't statically tell what kind of value is flowing through — same wire, three different semantics. A reader scanning the map sees one node shape but has to read the configuration to know what it does.

**4. Validation surface blows up.**
Three kinds today = three fixed shapes the validator checks. One unified kind = 2³ combinations of (literal? path? write_value?) the validator has to either accept or reject with a clear rule per combo. More edge-case bugs hide there.

**5. Canvas identity disappears.**
Today a green data box looks different from a blue call box at a glance. If one kind can be source-or-sink-or-constant, the badge stops telling the reader what's happening — they have to open the inspector.

---

**A middle ground I'd recommend**: split exactly along the natural seam — *sourcing* vs *sinking*.

- **`data` (source)** — emits one value. The value comes from either an inline literal typed in the inspector OR a file read at `path` (literal or wired). Same output port, same semantics: "here is the value." Choosing literal vs file is a low-stakes mode toggle because both end up as the same kind of thing on the wire.

- **`write` (sink)** — writes an input value to disk at `path`. Emits a done signal. Different action, different look on the canvas.

This collapses your three asks into two kinds while keeping the "I just want to type a value into a box" use case as a first-class feature of the source kind. Reading and emit-literal sit comfortably in one box because they answer the same question. Writing is its own thing because it does something fundamentally different (mutates the filesystem).

Want me to update 229 around that two-kind design (`data` with literal-or-file + `write`)?

### [903] type=user
ts: 2026-05-19T17:24:45.296Z

okay yeah let's do read and write instead of data and write. Read will always be trusted to output a value - write just outputs when it's done. Easily ignored or discarded if we don't care when it finishes. Can we make write be an orange cheddar color? nice and vivid but on the darker side.

read should hide / disable it's input port if a literal value is entered in the inspector.

read should detect the language used by each of it's output wires - remember, each output port can have multiple wires coming out of it. If there's three wires, one that goes to a C box, one that goes the a Lua box, and one that goes to a bash box, then we should output the value as close-to-natively as we can for that language. If there's multiple just do them in a for loop, for each wire. the downstream boxes should treat all read boxes as native.

write should do similar - it should detect which language the input wire is coming from, and use the correct writing procedure defined in the language spec to reduce the friction.

### [905] type=user
ts: 2026-05-19T17:25:26.296Z

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 317 — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	A data box (`kind: data` in box JSON) emits a value the rest of
9	the graph routes downstream. Today the runner reads the box's
10	`ref` file as raw bytes and emits them — there's no concept of
11	"authoring this datum in a particular language using that
12	language's natural structures." If you want to declare a Lua
13	table as a data box's value, you either:
14	
15	- Hand-translate the table to JSON, save it as a `.json` file,
16	  and lose the Lua syntax niceties; or
17	- Make it a call box that returns the table, paying for a full
18	  spec invocation just to read a constant; or
19	- Hard-code the bytes inline in the box's `data` field, with no
20	  syntactic help from any host language.
21	
22	This is the wrong shape. **A box author writes idiomatic code in
23	the language of their choice.** That's the rule 307 establishes
24	for call boxes: a C-box author writes a plain C function, no
25	SoraMech-specific glue. The data-box equivalent should hold —
26	a Lua-data-box author should write a Lua file that returns
27	whatever they want returned (a table, a string, a number), and
28	SoraMech should know how to lift that into the graph as a JSON
29	wire value without the author writing a single SoraMech-specific
30	character.
31	
32	## Intended behavior
33	
34	Every language spec gains two callbacks, declared in
35	`lang_spec_t` (issue 303):
36	
37	```c
38	/* Serialize a native-language value into JSON text.
39	 *
40	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle from spec.init.
41	 *   src       — caller-meaningful pointer to the native value.
42	 *               Format is spec-specific (see "Native handle
43	 *               representation" below).
44	 *   src_size  — size hint, or 0 if the spec interprets `src`
45	 *               without a length.
46	 *   out_buf   — destination buffer for JSON text (caller-owned).
47	 *   out_cap   — capacity of out_buf.
48	 *   out_size  — set to the bytes written.
49	 *
50	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. Writes an error message
51	 * via the spec's existing error-reporting plumbing. */
52	int (*native_to_json)(void *ctx,
53	                      const void *src, uint32_t src_size,
54	                      char *out_buf, uint32_t out_cap, uint32_t *out_size);
55	
56	/* Inverse: parse JSON text into a native-language value. The
57	 * native value is stored wherever the spec considers natural
58	 * (Lua: pushed to the worker's lua_State stack; C: written into
59	 * a typed struct supplied by `dst`; Bash: written as a textual
60	 * representation back into dst).
61	 *
62	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle.
63	 *   src       — JSON text.
64	 *   src_size  — JSON text length in bytes.
65	 *   dst       — caller-supplied target; semantics per spec.
66	 *   dst_cap   — capacity, where meaningful.
67	 *   dst_size  — set to the bytes / native units consumed.
68	 *
69	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. */
70	int (*json_to_native)(void *ctx,
71	                      const char *src, uint32_t src_size,
72	                      void *dst, uint32_t dst_cap, uint32_t *dst_size);
73	```
74	
75	These are the same fields 312's same-language fast path
76	reserves; this issue is the one that requires every spec to
77	**actually implement them**, driven by the data-box use case.
78	(312 needs them too, but its driver is performance; 317 needs
79	them for correctness — a data box can't function without them.)
80	
81	### The rule, restated
82	
83	The author of a data box writes a `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` /
84	`.<whatever>` file that returns or produces a value using
85	*their language's native idioms*. No SoraMech macros. No
86	SoraMech includes. No SoraMech-specific JSON shape constraints
87	beyond "what your spec's native_to_json can convert."
88	
89	If the author returns a Lua table, the Lua spec's
90	`native_to_json` converts it. If they return a C struct, the C
91	spec's `native_to_json` walks it according to the type declared
92	in the box. If they print JSON-ish text from a bash script, the
93	bash spec's `native_to_json` is a no-op identity (the script
94	already produced JSON).
95	
96	The contract belongs to the spec author, not the box author.
97	That's the inversion: **SoraMech bends to the language, not the
98	other way around.**
99	
100	## Native handle representation
101	
102	The shape of `src` / `dst` in the callbacks is per-spec. Each
103	spec documents what it accepts:
104	
105	- **Lua spec**: `src` is the index of a value already pushed
106	  onto the worker's `lua_State`. `src_size` is unused. The
107	  spec's `native_to_json` reads the value at that stack index
108	  and writes JSON. `json_to_native` pushes the parsed JSON value
109	  onto the stack, and `dst_size` reports the number of stack
110	  slots used (always 1 for SoraMech's flat-value model).
111	- **C spec**: `src` is a typed pointer + the box's declared
112	  output type drives the walk. `src_size` is the type's
113	  storage footprint. Mirror image for `json_to_native`. (This
114	  ties into 307's typed-wrapper-generation work — the same
115	  type system describes both the wrapper signature and the
116	  JSON shape.)
117	- **Bash spec**: `src` is text the script produced.
118	  `native_to_json` validates and reshapes to JSON if needed (a
119	  bash script that prints `42` becomes JSON `42`; a script that
120	  prints `{"a":1}` passes through unchanged). `json_to_native`
121	  is similarly a near-identity. The spec author is encouraged
122	  to require scripts produce well-formed JSON in the first
123	  place rather than write a bash-syntax parser.
124	
125	The dispatch layer doesn't know or care which representation a
126	spec uses. It only ever passes `src` and `src_size` to the
127	spec's callback. The spec is opaque to the runtime; that's the
128	whole point.
129	
130	## Data box semantics
131	
132	A data box's `data` field becomes optional. The box gains
133	optional `lang` and `ref` fields:
134	
135	```json
136	{
137	  "id": "config",
138	  "kind": "data",
139	  "lang": "lua",
140	  "ref": "config.lua"
141	}
142	```
143	
144	At graph load, the runner:
145	
146	1. Resolves the spec for `lang`.
147	2. Calls the spec's `init` (already done at worker startup).
148	3. At box-fire time, asks the spec to execute the `ref` file
149	   and produce a native value (this is essentially the same
150	   "load this file, run it, get its return value" path the
151	   spec already implements for call boxes — call boxes call a
152	   named function; data boxes take the file's return value).
153	4. Calls the spec's `native_to_json` to convert the native
154	   value to JSON text.
155	5. Pushes that JSON text down the wire as the box's output —
156	   same as today's data path, just with the value sourced
157	   from a language-native file.
158	
159	If the box has no `lang` and a literal `data` field, the legacy
160	inline-data path applies unchanged.
161	
162	## Why every spec must implement these
163	
164	The same JSON the data box emits is consumed by some downstream
165	box (call, comparator, file_write, whatever). That downstream
166	box, if it's in a different language, will eventually need
167	*its* spec's `json_to_native` to convert the JSON back. So the
168	two callbacks are paired — every spec that participates in the
169	graph needs both. A spec that implements `native_to_json` but
170	not `json_to_native` can only be data-box sources; a spec with
171	only `json_to_native` can only be call-box sinks. We require
172	both so any language can play any role.
173	
174	Specs that haven't been updated yet should set both callbacks
175	to a stub that returns -1 with a clear error message ("spec
176	`<name>`: native_to_json not implemented; this box's value
177	can't be routed cross-language"). The hard-fail makes the gap
178	loud rather than producing wrong output.
179	
180	## Suggested implementation steps
181	
182	1. Extend the `lang_spec_t` declaration in
183	   `langs/lang-spec.h` if not already done by 312's
184	   infrastructure work. (Per the round summary, the slot is
185	   reserved.)
186	2. Implement `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` in each
187	   spec:
188	   - **Lua spec**: walk the value at the stack index. Use the
189	     existing C JSON writer (`libs/json/json.c`). Handle Lua
190	     tables both as arrays (1-indexed contiguous integer keys)
191	     and as objects. Refuse functions, userdata, threads.
192	   - **C spec**: depends on 307's typed-wrapper machinery. For
193	     now, a stub that only supports scalar types (int, double,
194	     string) is enough to get data boxes working in C.
195	   - **Bash spec**: validate-and-pass-through. The script is
196	     expected to produce JSON. `native_to_json` runs the JSON
197	     parser to validate; `json_to_native` is identity.
198	3. Extend the data box runtime path in `src/012-dispatch.c`
199	   (or wherever data boxes dispatch from) to consult `lang` /
200	   `ref` when set and route through the spec callback chain.
201	4. Add a fixture: `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` with a Lua
202	   data box, a C consumer, and a Bash consumer all reading the
203	   same value. The integration check asserts each consumer
204	   sees the right data.
205	5. Update each spec's `*.info.md` to document the native handle
206	   shape its callbacks expect.
207	
208	## Relevant files
209	
210	- `langs/lang-spec.h` — extend the contract
211	- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` —
212	  implement the callbacks
213	- `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.info.md` — document native handle
214	  semantics per spec
215	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — data-box dispatch path
216	- `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` — new fixture (see step 4)
217	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — same author-writes-natural-
218	  code principle; this issue extends it from call boxes to data
219	  boxes
220	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — reuses the same
221	  spec callbacks for a different (performance) reason
222	
223	## Open questions
224	
225	- Bash's `json_to_native` is a near-identity because the
226	  scripts already deal in text. Is there value in having it
227	  validate and reformat for consistency? Probably yes; cheap
228	  insurance against malformed downstream input.
229	- Should the Lua spec auto-detect array-vs-object tables, or
230	  require the box author to annotate? Auto-detect (contiguous
231	  1-indexed integer keys → array; otherwise object) is what
232	  every other lua-to-json library does and is intuitive enough.
233	- C spec's `native_to_json` for arbitrary structs is a real
234	  rabbit hole. Tie scope to 307 — the same type declarations
235	  drive both the wrapper and the JSON walk. Until 307 lands,
236	  only scalar types are supported for C data boxes.
237	
```

Looks up the target box's language via its `ref` extension\n      (or the routing layer's pre-classified `lang` from issue 305).\n   b. Calls that language's `json_to_native` to lift the value\n      into the target language's native form.\n   c. Hands the native value to the target box's input slot.\n\nIf three wires go to a C box, a Lua box, and a Bash box, the\ndispatch loop runs the conversion three times — once per spec.\n**Downstream boxes treat the `read` box's output as native to\ntheir own language.** No SoraMech-specific glue at the consumer.\n\nFor files on disk, `read` treats the file contents as JSON text —\nthe canonical wire format — and feeds those bytes to each\ndownstream spec's `json_to_native`. A Lua user writing a file by\nhand still writes JSON (or uses a `write` box upstream, which\nproduces JSON anyway).\n\n### `write` per-upstream-wire dispatch\n\nA `write` box's `value` input is fed by exactly one upstream\nwire. The dispatch layer:\n\n1. Looks up the source box's language.\n2. Calls that language's `native_to_json` to canonicalize the\n   value to JSON text.\n3. Writes the JSON bytes to `path` atomically.\n\nIf the source is a Lua box returning a table, the Lua spec\nencodes the table to JSON. If the source is a C box returning a\nstruct, the C spec walks the type. The user never sees the\nserialization step — it's the spec's job, not the box author's.\n\n### Why this matters\n\nThe user's mental model is \"I write code in my language, the\ndata crosses the wire, the other side gets it in their\nlanguage.\" `read` and `write` should reinforce that, not break\nit. Either box would otherwise be the place where the user is\nforced to think about JSON shapes, byte order, or string\nescapes. With the spec-driven IO callbacks, all of that lives\nwhere it belongs — in the language specs.\n\n### Dependency relationship with 317\n\n229 cannot land cleanly without 317. The cross-language emit/write\nbehavior described above IS 317's contract; this issue just names\nthe runtime primitives that make first use of it. Implementation\norder: 317 → 229. If 229 lands first with a stub \"everything goes\nthrough bash-style text identity,\" the result is plain string IO\nwith no Lua-table / C-struct support, which is a regression from\nthe design goal.\n\n## Schema\n\n**read** (default file-source form):\n```json\n{\n  \"id\": \"load_cfg\",\n  \"kind\": \"read\",\n  \"inputs\": [{ \"name\": \"path\" }],\n  \"ui\": { \"x\": 100, \"y\": 100 }\n}\n```\n\n**read** with inline literal value (no path port):\n```json\n{\n  \"id\": \"default_prompt\",\n  \"kind\": \"read\",\n  \"value\": \"You are a helpful assistant.\",\n  \"ui\": { \"x\": 100, \"y\": 100 }\n}\n```\n\n**read** with inline literal path (path port still present\non canvas if no `value` is set, but the port's label shows\nthe literal — same convention as issue 235):\n```json\n{\n  \"id\": \"load_cfg\",\n  \"kind\": \"read\",\n  \"inputs\": [{ \"name\": \"path\", \"value\": \"config.json\" }],\n  \"ui\": { \"x\": 100, \"y\": 100 }\n}\n```\n\n**write**:\n```json\n{\n  \"id\": \"save_result\",\n  \"kind\": \"write\",\n  \"inputs\": [\n    { \"name\": \"path\" },\n    { \"name\": \"value\" }\n  ],\n  \"ui\": { \"x\": 400, \"y\": 100 }\n}\n```\n\n## Inspector behavior\n\n### `read`\n\n- **Inline value** field (textarea / text input): a free-form\n  literal that, when non-empty, becomes the box's output and\n  HIDES the `path` input port on the canvas. Same hide-when-\n  literal precedence is the obvious progression of issue 235's\n  \"literal value replaces port name\" idea, applied to the port\n  itself.\n- **Path** field: the literal-on-port equivalent for the `path`\n  input. Disabled when the inline value is set.\n- The two fields are mutually-illuminating: typing into one\n  visibly disables the other so the user always knows which\n  mode the box is in.\n\n### `write`\n\n- Two input port rows (`path`, `value`) with the standard\n  port literal / wire UI.\n- No inline-value field (a `write` with no value to commit\n  is meaningless).\n\n### Kind dropdown\n\nReplace the current `['call', 'data']` list with\n`['call', 'read', 'write']`. Picking a kind from this dropdown\nswaps the box's input/output shape per the schemas above.\n\n## Migration\n\nLegacy `data` boxes (the cosmetic-only kind) get rewritten by\nthe loader. Two paths:\n\n- Boxes with a `ref` / `fn` (the call-equivalent shape they\n  carry today) → rewrite `kind` to `call`. Behavior identical\n  to current.\n- Boxes without a `ref` / `fn` (rare — would be invalid today)\n  → rewrite to `read` with no path; the validator then flags\n  them so the user notices and fills in a path or literal.\n\nThe `data` kind is retired entirely. The schema rejects it\nafter migration runs once.\n\n## Unwired-output discard rule\n\nThe general rule this issue makes use of, applicable to every\nbox:\n\n> **If an output value is produced but no wire carries it\n> downstream, the value is discarded.**\n\nA `write` box's done boolean is the most visible case — a user\nwho doesn't need a chain-after-write trigger leaves the output\nunwired and the runtime drops the value. The rule already holds\ninformally for every box; documenting it here (and in\n`docs/001-architecture.md`) makes it explicit.\n\n## Suggested implementation sequence\n\n1. **Block on 317**: cross-language emit/write callbacks must be\n   in place. Without them this issue degrades to plain-text IO.\n2. **Schema** (`src/001-schema.lua`, `src/010-graph-loader.c`):\n   accept `read` and `write`; retire `data`.\n3. **Loader migration** (`src/003-loader.lua`): rewrite legacy\n   `data` boxes per the migration section.\n4. **Phase 2 executor** (`src/004-executor.lua`): handle `read`\n   (literal or file) and `write` directly. Cross-language\n   conversion uses the spec callbacks via the same path as\n   call-box value transport.\n5. **Phase 3 dispatch** (`src/012-dispatch.c`): same handling at\n   the dispatch layer. `read`'s fan-out loops through downstream\n   wires per spec; `write` looks up source-spec on its `value`\n   input.\n6. **Inspector** (`assets/js/004-inspector.js`): kind-specific\n   layout per \"Inspector behavior\" above. The inline-value field\n   for `read` is new; the port-hide-when-literal-value behavior\n   is new and needs a small render-side hook in `draw_box`.\n7. **Box rendering** (`assets/js/002-boxes.js`):\n   - Add `read: '#4caf7d'` and `write: '#d4762a'` to `KIND_COLOR`.\n   - Add `read`/`write` to the `KIND_HEADER` map from issue 236.\n   - Honor `hide_port` flag (or equivalent) on the path input\n     when the inline value is set on a `read` box.\n8. **Documentation**: update `docs/001-architecture.md` with the\n   new kinds, the unwired-output rule, and the language-aware\n   IO behavior. Update the dispatch-layer info.md.\n\n## Open questions\n\n- **Output port labels** for `read` and `write`: `contents` for\n  read, `done` for write — pick once, document, move on. Issue\n  236 already established the per-kind output-label table; add\n  these two rows.\n- **Static path on `write`**: should `write` also accept an\n  inline literal path on its `path` port (same way `read`\n  does)? Probably yes — the use case \"save to a fixed location\n  every time\" is common. Comes free if we apply the existing\n  port-literal-value mechanism.\n- **`read` with both literal and path set on disk** (a user who\n  edits the JSON by hand and sets both): validator rejects, or\n  literal-wins? Validator rejects — explicit error rather than\n  hidden precedence.\n- **Cross-language type fidelity** for the C spec: walking\n  arbitrary C structs is rabbit-hole territory (issue 317\n  tracks this). Until C's `native_to_json` handles structs,\n  C-source `write` boxes are limited to scalar values. Same\n  limitation issue 317 documents.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `src/001-schema.lua` — kind validation, new shapes\n- `src/010-graph-loader.c` — C-side schema, migration\n- `src/003-loader.lua` — legacy `data` rewrite\n- `src/004-executor.lua` — phase 2 runtime handling\n- `src/012-dispatch.c` — phase 3 dispatch primitives\n- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — kind-specific inspector layout\n- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — color, header, port-hide on literal\n- `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.c` — `native_to_json` / `json_to_native`\n  (issue 317 owns these)\n- `docs/001-architecture.md` — kinds section, unwired-output rule\n- `issues/317-spec-json-bridge-for-data-boxes.md` — hard dependency\n- `issues/235-...completed/` — literal-replaces-port-name idea\n  extended to \"literal hides port entirely\" here\n- `issues/236-...completed/` — per-kind header/output-label map\n- `issues/completed/216-read-file-box.md` — Lua-side read superseded\n- `issues/304-task-dispatch-layer.md` — phase 3 home for these\n- `issues/completed/109-data-files-persistent-and-ephemeral.md` —\n  `soramech-data` is a separate concern; not affected\n\n---\n\n## Historical proposals (superseded)\n\n### Revision 2 (single `data` kind, literal-or-file)\n\nProposed one unified source box: literal value typed in the\ninspector OR file at `path`. Plus a separate `write` sink.\nRejected because the source-and-sink-in-one-kind ambiguity\nanalysis (above, in \"Why two kinds\") flagged real footguns —\nthe `path`-input-without-`value` case silently reading from\na file the user meant to write to.\n\n### Revision 1 (`data` source + `file_write` sink)\n\nProposed a `data` kind (no inputs, just a `path` field; emits\nfile bytes) and a separate write-sink. Renamed in revision 2\nto `read` / `write` because verbs match what the boxes do\nbetter than `data` does. Both revisions kept the same source\n/ sink split.\n\nThe binary-data, atomic-write, JSON-parsing, and dispatch-layer\nhome questions all carry over unchanged through every\nrevision.\n",

ts: 2026-05-19T17:31:00.901Z

Issue 229 is updated. Key points captured:

**Two kinds**: `read` + `write`. No unified `data` source.

**`read`**:
- Default: `path` input → reads file, emits contents.
- Inline literal value in inspector → emits that, hides the `path` input port on canvas.
- Per-downstream-wire native conversion: for each outgoing wire, look up the target box's language and use that spec's `json_to_native` to lift the value to native form. A Lua consumer gets a Lua table; a C consumer gets a C struct; a Bash consumer gets text.

**`write`**:
- Inputs: `path` + `value`. Output: `true` boolean done signal (optional, discarded when unwired).
- Per-upstream-wire native conversion: ask the source language's spec to canonicalize via `native_to_json`, write JSON bytes atomically.
- Color: orange cheddar (`#d4762a`).

**Dependency**: 229 needs 317 (the spec JSON bridge) for the per-language work. Implementation order: 317 → 229.

**Migration**: legacy `data` kind gets rewritten to `call` (where it carries ref/fn) or `read` (where it doesn't). `data` is retired.

229 stays open. Not implementing yet because:

1. It depends on 317 (open, not started).
2. 317 itself depends on infrastructure the other dev is actively building in phase 3 (`langs/<x>/spec.c` callbacks, dispatch layer).
3. Doing the editor + executor surface now without the spec callbacks would lock in a stub design that 317 then has to undo.

I'd commit the 229 redesign as a doc-only change and revisit after 317 lands. Want me to commit the spec update?

About half the radius of a wired port's dot.\n  Visual marker only — it advertises \"this port is bound\" the\n  same way the regular dot advertises \"this port can be wired.\"\n  Color match its kind, same as the regular dot, so the eye\n  reads it as a port without confusion.\n- **Literal value** (`\"hello world\"`) — to the RIGHT of the\n  nodule, same position the regular port name occupies on a\n  wired port. Truncation rules from 235 still apply (24-char\n  limit with ellipsis, tooltip for the full value).\n\nA wired port stays exactly as it is today — full-size dot,\nname to its right, no left-of-box-edge label.\n\n### Wire-connect targeting disabled\n\nA literal-bound port is no longer a valid drag-target for\nwires. `Boxes.hit_test_port` excludes literal-bound input ports\nfrom its hit search, so:\n\n- The cursor over a nodule does NOT highlight the port for a\n  wire-grab.\n- The user cannot start drawing a wire from / to a literal-bound\n  port. _tool_result:_
```
1	# 230 — Required inputs, the optional flag, and no-null-from-missing
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	A box's input port can be left without a wire and without a literal
9	value. The phase 2 executor runs the box anyway; the function on
10	the other end gets `nil` (Lua), unset (Bash), or whatever default
11	the language hands a missing positional arg. Errors only surface if
12	the function happens to reference the missing arg in a way the
13	language can't tolerate (e.g. `string.upper(nil)` in Lua).
14	
15	This is a class of silent-error bug. The user had a wiring intent,
16	forgot to plug something in, and the runtime quietly papered over
17	the mistake.
18	
19	## Intended behavior
20	
21	Every input port is in one of three states by **compile time**:
22	
23	1. A wire is connected to it.
24	2. A literal value is set on it.
25	3. It carries `optional: true` on the port record.
26	
27	**Compile fails** when any port is in none of those states. The
28	error is precise: which box, which port. The Compile button (issue
29	222) is the natural enforcement point, with the same check
30	duplicating in the phase 3 graph loader (issue 305).
31	
32	### "Optional" is not "null"
33	
34	`optional: true` says "this argument doesn't have to be passed." It
35	does not mean "pass null." At the spec boundary, an optional
36	unwired un-littered port produces a shorter arg list — the language
37	sees fewer arguments, not a null argument. Each language handles
38	that natively:
39	
40	- **Lua**: omitted args are `nil`. That's a language convention,
41	  not a wire-level value. Inside the Lua function, `arg or default`
42	  works.
43	- **Bash**: a positional arg that wasn't passed is unset.
44	  `${arg:-default}` provides a default.
45	- **C**: the spec's compile callback (issue 307) generates a wrapper
46	  that reads `n_inputs` and only forwards the args that are present.
47	
48	### Null as an explicit value (allowed)
49	
50	If the user needs to pass null because some downstream library
51	expects it, they can — by typing the literal `null` into the value
52	field. That's a string on the wire encoded as JSON null. Each spec
53	maps it to its language's native null on the way in:
54	
55	| JSON value  | Lua    | Bash       | C       |
56	|-------------|--------|------------|---------|
57	| `null`      | nil    | empty `""` | `NULL`  |
58	| `42`        | 42     | `"42"`     | 42      |
59	| `"foo"`     | `foo`  | `foo`      | `"foo"` |
60	| `true`      | true   | `"true"`   | `1`     |
61	
62	The crucial distinction: **explicit null arrives through the
63	function call as a real argument**; **optional-and-absent does not
64	arrive at all**. Bash empty-string-vs-unset disappears in the
65	optional path because the arg position simply isn't passed.
66	
67	### Schema
68	
69	Add `optional` as an optional boolean on input port records:
70	
71	```json
72	{
73	  "name": "config_path",
74	  "type": "string",
75	  "optional": true
76	}
77	```
78	
79	Absence means `false`. No migration; existing maps work unchanged.
80	
81	## Compile-time check
82	
83	The check walks every box, every input. For each:
84	- If the port has `value` set (literal): pass.
85	- If any connection has `to_box === box.id && to_input === port.name`:
86	  pass.
87	- If `port.optional === true`: pass.
88	- Otherwise: error, with a precise message:
89	  `"box <id>: input '<port>' has no wire, no literal, and is not optional"`
90	
91	The check runs:
92	- **Editor** — on Compile button click (issue 222 → 309). Errors
93	  surface in the editor without writing the compiled output.
94	- **Loader** — at run time (phase 3 issue 305). Errors abort the
95	  run. Belt and suspenders: the editor catches it but a hand-edited
96	  map JSON can still trip the loader check.
97	
98	## Spec API for "is this arg present?"
99	
100	The spec doesn't need to ask. The dispatch layer assembles
101	`input_data[]` and `input_sizes[]` from the slots that actually
102	have values. An optional port that's absent isn't in those arrays;
103	`n_inputs` is shorter by one. The spec invokes the language with
104	the args it received. The language's native call convention does
105	the rest.
106	
107	For C specifically, the compile-callback-generated wrapper takes
108	`int n_inputs` and a count-prefixed array. The wrapper picks the
109	first M args that match its declared parameters and forwards them;
110	trailing optionals get omitted from the call (or filled with C's
111	zero-valued types, depending on the wrapper convention). This is
112	issue 307's territory.
113	
114	## Inspector UI
115	
116	A small per-port flag in the inspector's port block:
117	
118	```
119	[name] [var] [opt]
120	[ value ........... ]
121	```
122	
123	`opt` toggles `port.optional` on the port record. Visual: same
124	small button as `var` but distinct color.
125	
126	## Open questions
127	
128	- **Whether to allow optional + literal**: yes. A literal is an
129	  explicit value; the optional flag is irrelevant when the value
130	  is set. The compile check just needs all three signals
131	  available; literal wins over optional.
132	- **What syntax means "explicit null literal"**: lean toward
133	  literally typing `null` (case-insensitive) in the value field.
134	  An ambiguity arises when the user actually wants the string
135	  `"null"` — escape it as `"null"` (with quotes) which the value
136	  parser interprets as a string literal. Worth nailing down before
137	  shipping.
138	
139	## Relevant files
140	
141	- `src/001-schema.lua` — accept `optional` field
142	- `src/003-loader.lua` (phase 2) — compile-time check
143	- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — `opt` toggle in port block
144	- `assets/js/005-app.js` — Compile button hooks check before
145	  compile
146	- `issues/309-build-system.md` — compile/package step calls the
147	  check
148	- `issues/305-c-graph-loader.md` — phase 3 check at load time
149	- `issues/303-language-runtime-spec.md` — `invoke`'s n_inputs
150	  reflects what was actually passed
151	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — wrapper handles short-arg case
152	- `issues/308-bash-language-spec.md` — null vs unset distinction
153	  evaporates in the optional path
154	
```

1	-- Schema definitions and validators for SoraMech map files.
2	-- Validates box files, meta.json, and drivers.json against the spec
3	-- in docs/001-architecture.md. Used by both the runner and the server
4	-- before any file is written to disk.
5	
6	local DIR = "/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech"
7	
8	local M = {}
9	
10	-- {{{ valid_kinds
11	local valid_kinds = { call = true, data = true }
12	-- }}}
13	
14	-- {{{ valid_branches
15	-- null/nil means no comparator (single wire); the three strings are comparator outputs.
16	local valid_branches = { lt = true, eq = true, gt = true }
17	-- }}}
18	
19	-- {{{ err
20	local function err(errors, msg)
21	    errors[#errors + 1] = msg
22	end
23	-- }}}
24	
25	-- {{{ validate_connection
26	local function validate_connection(c, i, errors)
27	    -- from_branch is nil (no comparator) or one of "lt"/"eq"/"gt"
28	    if c.from_box == nil then
29	        err(errors, "connection[" .. i .. "] missing 'from_box'")
30	    end
31	    if c.from_branch ~= nil and not valid_branches[c.from_branch] then
32	        err(errors, "connection[" .. i .. "] 'from_branch' must be nil, 'lt', 'eq', or 'gt'")
33	    end
34	    if c.to_box == nil then
35	        err(errors, "connection[" .. i .. "] missing 'to_box'")
36	    end
37	    if c.to_input == nil then
38	        err(errors, "connection[" .. i .. "] missing 'to_input'")
39	    end
40	end
41	-- }}}
42	
43	-- {{{ validate_box
44	function M.validate_box(box)
45	    local errors = {}
46	    if type(box) ~= "table" then
47	        return { "box must be a table" }
48	    end
49	
50	    if box.id == nil or type(box.id) ~= "string" then
51	        err(errors, "missing or non-string 'id'")
52	    end
53	    if box.label == nil or type(box.label) ~= "string" then
54	        err(errors, "missing or non-string 'label'")
55	    end
56	    if box.kind == nil or not valid_kinds[box.kind] then
57	        err(errors, "missing or invalid 'kind' (must be 'call' or 'data')")
58	    end
59	
60	    if box.kind == "call" or box.kind == "data" then
61	        -- iterator boxes (issue 221) are a pure routing primitive: input
62	        -- copies straight to a chosen output, no language function is
63	        -- invoked. Such a box has no ref/fn — only `iterator_outputs`,
64	        -- an ordered list of output slot names. Otherwise ref is required.
65	        local is_iterator = box.iterator_outputs ~= nil
66	        if not is_iterator then
67	            if box.ref == nil or type(box.ref) ~= "string" then
68	                err(errors, "box missing 'ref'")
69	            end
70	        end
71	        if box.iterator_outputs ~= nil then
72	            if type(box.iterator_outputs) ~= "table" then
73	                err(errors, "'iterator_outputs' must be an array")
74	            else
75	                for i, name in ipairs(box.iterator_outputs) do
76	                    if type(name) ~= "string" then
77	                        err(errors, "'iterator_outputs[" .. i .. "]' must be a string")
78	                    end
79	                end
80	            end
81	        end
82	        -- fn is optional for binary callers (shell scripts, binaries)
83	        if box.inputs ~= nil and type(box.inputs) ~= "table" then
84	            err(errors, "'inputs' must be an array")
85	        end
86	        -- variadic_inputs: optional list of base names; each name must
87	        -- correspond to a series of inputs named "<base>_<index>" in
88	        -- box.inputs (the editor maintains this; we only check the
89	        -- shape here). See issue 217 part B.
90	        if box.variadic_inputs ~= nil then
91	            if type(box.variadic_inputs) ~= "table" then
92	                err(errors, "'variadic_inputs' must be an array")
93	            else
94	                for i, name in ipairs(box.variadic_inputs) do
95	                    if type(name) ~= "string" then
96	                        err(errors, "'variadic_inputs[" .. i .. "]' must be a string")
97	                    end
98	                end
99	            end
100	        end
101	        -- comparand: optional; if present it must be a string parseable as number at runtime
102	        if box.comparand ~= nil and type(box.comparand) ~= "string" then
103	            err(errors, "'comparand' must be a string")
104	        end
105	        -- has_output: optional; absence means "true" (default behavior).
106	        -- false marks a sink box whose function has no return values
107	        -- (issue 226); the runtime treats such boxes as terminal and the
108	        -- editor hides the output port and inspector output section.
109	        if box.has_output ~= nil and type(box.has_output) ~= "boolean" then
110	            err(errors, "'has_output' must be a boolean")
111	        end
112	        if box.connections ~= nil then
113	            for i, c in ipairs(box.connections) do
114	                validate_connection(c, i, errors)
115	            end
116	        end
117	    end
118	
119	    if box.ui ~= nil then
120	        if type(box.ui) ~= "table" then
121	            err(errors, "'ui' must be a table")
122	        elseif box.ui.x == nil or box.ui.y == nil then
123	            err(errors, "'ui' must have 'x' and 'y' fields")
124	        end
125	    end
126	
127	    return errors
128	end
129	-- }}}
130	
131	-- {{{ validate_meta
132	function M.validate_meta(meta)
133	    local errors = {}
134	    if type(meta) ~= "table" then
135	        return { "meta.json must be a table" }
136	    end
137	    if meta.name == nil or type(meta.name) ~= "string" then
138	        err(errors, "meta.json missing 'name'")
139	    end
140	    if meta.entry_box_id == nil or type(meta.entry_box_id) ~= "string" then
141	        err(errors, "meta.json missing 'entry_box_id'")
142	    end
143	    return errors
144	end
145	-- }}}
146	
147	-- {{{ validate_drivers
148	function M.validate_drivers(drivers)
149	    local errors = {}
150	    if type(drivers) ~= "table" then
151	        return { "drivers.json must be a table" }
152	    end
153	    for ext, path in pairs(drivers) do
154	        if type(ext) ~= "string" or ext:sub(1, 1) ~= "." then
155	            err(errors, "driver key '" .. tostring(ext) .. "' must be a dot-prefixed extension")
156	        end
157	        if type(path) ~= "string" then
158	            err(errors, "driver value for '" .. ext .. "' must be a string path")
159	        end
160	    end
161	    return errors
162	end
163	-- }}}
164	
165	return M
166	
```

560	  // {{{ mk_port_display
561	  // Renders input ports as a two-row block per port: name field +
562	  // variadic toggle on the top row, value field on the bottom row.
563	  // Layout chosen so the value (often the longest entry) gets the
564	  // full sidebar width instead of competing for space with the name
565	  // (issue 224 reverted: name editing comes back to the inspector
566	  // because the canvas <input> overlay was eating drag attempts).
567	  function mk_port_display(ports) {
568	    const wrap = document.createElement('div');
569	    wrap.className = 'port-display';
570	    if (!ports || ports.length === 0) {
571	      const empty = document.createElement('span');
572	      empty.className   = 'port-display-empty';
573	      empty.textContent = '(none)';
574	      wrap.appendChild(empty);
575	    } else {
576	      ports.forEach((p, i) => {
577	        const block = document.createElement('div');
578	        block.className = 'port-display-block';
579	
580	        const top_row = document.createElement('div');
581	        top_row.className = 'port-display-row';
582	
583	        // Variadic context (issue 217 part B):
584	        //   in_var_group = is part of a variadic group (one of multiple slots)
585	        //   pv           = { base, index } parsed from the slot name
586	        const pv           = current_box ? parse_variadic_name(p.name) : null;
587	        const in_var_group = current_box && is_variadic_slot(current_box, p.name);
588	
589	        // Read-only name label. Port names come from the parsed
590	        // function signature (file browser populates them); editing
591	        // them in the inspector is gone — the original 208 rationale
592	        // (prevent typo drift between source and box JSON) holds, and
593	        // post-fact rename was just a user tag anyway.
594	        const name_el = document.createElement('span');
595	        name_el.className   = 'port-name-display';
596	        name_el.textContent = p.name || '';
597	        top_row.appendChild(name_el);
598	
599	        if (p.type && p.type !== 'any') {
600	          const type_el = document.createElement('span');
601	          type_el.className   = 'port-type';
602	          type_el.textContent = ':' + p.type;
603	          top_row.appendChild(type_el);
604	        }
605	
606	        // Variadic control button. Three states:
607	        //   1. Plain port — "var" toggle turns it on
608	        //   2. First slot of a variadic group — "var ×" collapses back
609	        //   3. Non-first slot — "×" removes that slot
610	        const btn_style = 'background:none;border:1px solid #2a2f45;border-radius:3px;' +
611	          'color:#6c72a0;cursor:pointer;font-family:monospace;font-size:9px;' +
612	          'padding:1px 5px;line-height:1.4;';
613	
614	        if (!in_var_group) {
615	          const var_btn = document.createElement('button');
616	          var_btn.style.cssText = btn_style;
617	          var_btn.textContent   = 'var';
618	          var_btn.title         = 'mark this input as variadic (grows to N slots)';
619	          var_btn.onclick       = () => make_variadic(p.name);
620	          top_row.appendChild(var_btn);
621	        } else if (pv.index === 0) {
622	          const var_btn = document.createElement('button');
623	          var_btn.style.cssText = btn_style + 'border-color:#4a9eff;color:#4a9eff;';
624	          var_btn.textContent   = 'var ×';
625	          var_btn.title         = 'collapse back to a single non-variadic input';
626	          var_btn.onclick       = () => unmake_variadic(pv.base);
627	          top_row.appendChild(var_btn);
628	        } else {
629	          const x_btn = document.createElement('button');
630	          x_btn.style.cssText = btn_style;
631	          x_btn.textContent   = '×';
632	          x_btn.title         = 'remove this slot';
633	          x_btn.onclick       = () => remove_variadic_slot(p.name);
634	          top_row.appendChild(x_btn);
635	        }
636	
637	        block.appendChild(top_row);
638	
639	        // Value input — full-width row below the name. Empty means
640	        // "no literal" (wire supplies the value at runtime).
641	        const val_inp = document.createElement('input');
642	        val_inp.type        = 'text';
643	        val_inp.value       = p.value !== undefined ? String(p.value) : '';
644	        val_inp.placeholder = 'value…';
645	        val_inp.className   = 'port-val-inp port-val-inp-wide';
646	        val_inp.addEventListener('input', async () => {
647	          const new_val = val_inp.value === '' ? undefined : val_inp.value;
648	          ports[i].value = new_val;

36	local function run_task(box, inputs, drivers, map_dir)
37	    local ref = box.ref
38	    local fn  = box.fn
39	
40	    -- locate the driver for this ref
41	    local driver = driver_path_for(ref, drivers)
42	    if not driver then
43	        return nil, "no driver found for ref '" .. ref .. "'"
44	    end
45	
46	    -- resolve ref relative to map_dir
47	    local abs_ref = map_dir .. "/" .. ref
48	
49	    -- build argument list: each input port's value as a JSON-encoded shell arg
50	    local args = {}
51	    for _, port in ipairs(box.inputs or {}) do
52	        local val = inputs[port.name]
53	        if val == nil then
54	            -- nil input becomes JSON null
55	            args[#args + 1] = shell_quote(json.encode(nil))
56	        else
57	            args[#args + 1] = shell_quote(json.encode(val))
58	        end
59	    end
60	
61	    local cmd
62	    if fn then
63	        -- source caller: driver file fn arg_count [args...]
64	        cmd = driver .. " " .. shell_quote(abs_ref) .. " " ..
65	              shell_quote(fn) .. " " .. #args
66	        for _, a in ipairs(args) do cmd = cmd .. " " .. a end
67	    else
68	        -- binary caller: run ref directly with args
69	        cmd = abs_ref .. " " .. #args
70	        for _, a in ipairs(args) do cmd = cmd .. " " .. a end
71	    end
72	
73	    -- invoke driver; capture stdout; errors go to stderr (visible in terminal)
74	    local tmpfile = map_dir .. "/tmp/driver-stderr-" .. box.id .. ".txt"
75	    local full_cmd = "sh -c " .. shell_quote(cmd .. " 2>" .. tmpfile)
76	    local ph = io.popen(full_cmd, "r")
77	    local stdout = ph:read("*a")
78	    local ok_close = ph:close()
79	
80	    -- read stderr if any
81	    local sf = io.open(tmpfile, "r")
82	    local stderr_text = sf and sf:read("*a") or ""
83	    if sf then sf:close() end
84	    os.remove(tmpfile)
85	
86	    if not ok_close then
87	        local err_msg = "driver failed for box '" .. box.id .. "'"
88	        if stderr_text and #stderr_text > 0 then
89	            err_msg = err_msg .. ": " .. stderr_text:gsub("%s+$", "")
90	        end
91	        return nil, err_msg
92	    end
93	
94	    -- parse single JSON value from stdout; one output wire per box
95	    local output_val, _, jerr = json.decode(stdout)
96	    if jerr then
97	        return nil, "box '" .. box.id .. "': driver stdout is not valid JSON: " ..
98	            tostring(jerr) .. " (got: " .. tostring(stdout):sub(1, 80) .. ")"
99	    end
100	
101	    return output_val, nil
102	end
103	-- }}}
104	
105	-- {{{ inputs_satisfied
106	local function inputs_satisfied(box, store)
107	    -- a box is ready when all its declared input ports have a value in the store
108	    for _, port in ipairs(box.inputs or {}) do
109	        if store[box.id .. "." .. port.name] == nil then
110	            return false
111	        end
112	    end
113	    return true
114	end
115	-- }}}
```

  "new_string":"-- {{{ check_input_bindings\n-- Graph-level check from issue 230: every input port on every box\n-- must be in one of three states by compile time —\n--   1. a wire is connected to it\n--   2. a literal `value` is set on it\n--   3. it carries `optional: true`\n-- Otherwise the box would silently receive a missing arg at runtime\n-- and the bug would only surface where the function happens to\n-- reference it. The check fails loud and points at the exact box +\n-- port instead.\n--\n-- `boxes` is a table mapping box-id → box record (the shape the\n-- editor cache and the phase 2 loader's graph use). Returns an\n-- array of error strings; empty array means OK.\nfunction M.check_input_bindings(boxes)\n    local errors = {}\n\n    -- precompute: for each (to_box, to_input) → true if a wire targets it.\n    -- Connections live on both endpoints (issue 228), so scanning every\n    -- box's connection list overcounts but doesn't miss anything.\n    local wired = {}\n    for _, box in pairs(boxes) do\n        for _, c in ipairs(box.connections or {}) do\n            if c.to_box and c.to_input then\n                wired[c.to_box .. \"\\0\" .. c.to_input] = true\n            end\n        end\n    end\n\n    -- stable per-box iteration for predictable error ordering\n    local ids = {}\n    for id, _ in pairs(boxes) do ids[#ids + 1] = id end\n    table.sort(ids)\n\n    for _, id in ipairs(ids) do\n        local box = boxes[id]\n        for _, p in ipairs(box.inputs or {}) do\n            local has_wire    = wired[id .. \"\\0\" .. p.name] == true\n            local has_literal = p.value ~= nil and p.value ~= \"\"\n            local is_optional = p.optional == true\n            if not (has_wire or has_literal or is_optional) then\n                err(errors, \"box '\" .. id .. \"': input '\" .. tostring(p.name) ..\n                    \"' has no wire, no literal, and is not optional\")\n            end\n        end\n    end\n\n    return errors\nend\n-- }}}\n\n-- {{{ validate_meta",

72	local function check_connections(boxes, errors)
73	    -- each connection is stored in BOTH endpoint box files as the same 4-field record.
74	    -- we only process connections where from_box == this box's id (outgoing connections).
75	    -- incoming reciprocal records (from_box != id) are skipped here; they are validated
76	    -- implicitly when we process the source box.
77	    for id, box in pairs(boxes) do
78	        local conns = box.connections or {}
79	        for _, c in ipairs(conns) do
80	            if c.from_box ~= id then
81	                -- this is the reciprocal copy stored for reference; skip
82	                goto next_conn
83	            end
84	
85	            local target = boxes[c.to_box]
86	            if target == nil then
87	                errors[#errors + 1] = "box '" .. id .. "': connection to unknown box '" ..
88	                    tostring(c.to_box) .. "'"
89	            else
90	                -- find exact match in target: all four fields must agree
91	                local found = false
92	                for _, tc in ipairs(target.connections or {}) do
93	                    if tc.from_box    == c.from_box   and
94	                       tc.from_output == c.from_output and
95	                       tc.to_box      == c.to_box      and
96	                       tc.to_input    == c.to_input    then
97	                        found = true
98	                        break
99	                    end
100	                end
101	                if not found then
102	                    errors[#errors + 1] = "box '" .. id .. "': connection " ..
103	                        c.from_box .. "." .. c.from_output ..
104	                        " -> " .. c.to_box .. "." .. c.to_input ..
105	                        " not found in target box file (both ends must store the same record)"
106	                end
107	            end
108	
109	            ::next_conn::
110	        end
111	    end
112	end
113	-- }}}
114

116	local function check_branch_else(boxes, errors)
117	    -- branch boxes must have an "else" port (enforced by schema, re-checked here
118	    -- for clarity), and numeric/string-predicate branch boxes must wire "else"
119	    for id, box in pairs(boxes) do
120	        if box.kind ~= "branch" then goto continue end
121	
122	        local has_else_port = false
123	        local has_else_wire = false
124	        for _, p in ipairs(box.ports or {}) do
125	            if p.name == "else" then
126	                has_else_port = true
127	            end
128	        end
129	        for _, c in ipairs(box.connections or {}) do
130	            if c.from_port == "else" then
131	                has_else_wire = true
132	            end
133	        end
134	
135	        if not has_else_port then
136	            errors[#errors + 1] = "branch box '" .. id .. "' missing 'else' port"
137	        end
138	
139	        -- if else is unwired, the box must have retry_vary upstream to be valid;
140	        -- we cannot check retry_vary here without traversing upstream, so we emit
141	        -- a warning rather than a hard error — the runner will enforce at execute time
142	        if has_else_port and not has_else_wire then
143	            errors[#errors + 1] = "branch box '" .. id ..
144	                "': 'else' port is unwired (valid only for LLM retry; runner will " ..
145	                "require upstream box to have retry_vary defined)"
146	        end
147	
148	        ::continue::
149	    end

### [1040] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:50:12.164Z

The `var` button has three states depending on which port you click it on:

| port state | button | what clicking does |
|---|---|---|
| plain port (e.g. `text`) | `var` | Convert to a variadic group with one slot. The port is renamed `text_0`. |
| first slot of a group (`text_0`) | `var ×` | Collapse the whole group back to a single port. Drops slots 1+, renames `text_0` → `text`. |
| any later slot (`text_1`, `text_2`, …) | `×` | Remove just that slot. Higher slots rename down (`text_2` → `text_1`, etc). |

The two automatic behaviors layered on top:

- **Auto-grow**: when you wire to the *last* slot of a variadic group, the editor immediately adds a new empty `text_N+1` slot. Same thing fires when you set a literal value on the last slot. So you never have to click `var` more than once — just keep wiring and the slots appear under you.
- **No auto-shrink**: disconnecting a wire or clearing a literal doesn't remove the slot. The `×` button is the only way to delete a slot. This was a deliberate call from 217 so transient editing (unplug, replug) doesn't lose your structure.

On the function side, what makes this work is the language's native variadic syntax — `function M.concat(sep, ...)` in Lua, `"$@"` after a shift in Bash, `argc` / `argv` for a C box. The editor's `text_0`, `text_1`, `text_2`, … map onto the positional args after any fixed-name inputs (`sep` in the concat example stays as a regular, non-variadic input).

A common use case: `libs/text.lua`'s `M.concat(sep, ...)` — `sep` is plain, `text` you click `var` on, and now you can keep wiring `text_0`, `text_1`, `text_2` from whichever sources you want them joined from. The whole row collapses to one literal join at runtime.

### [1045] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:54:57.163Z

It does the silent-wrong thing today, and there's no guardrail.

What actually happens at runtime:

- Lua / Bash silently swallow the extra args. If your function is `function M.upper(text)` and the editor sends three args from `text_0`, `text_1`, `text_2`, Lua treats `text = text_0_val` and drops the rest. Same shape with Bash — extra `$N` arguments exist but the function body never references them.
- C is worse — if the wrapper's `n_inputs` doesn't match the declared parameter count, you'd typically get an error from the wrapper, but the exact behavior depends on what 307's wrapper convention ends up being.

So the user wires up what looks like a five-input concat, three slots silently never reach the function, and the only signal is "the output is wrong." That's the same silent-error class 230 just fixed for missing-required-inputs, but in the other direction.

**The editor doesn't check.** Today the `var` toggle is purely editor-side state — it changes the box JSON shape (slot names, the `variadic_inputs` list) and the runtime assembles positional args from it, but nothing cross-references the underlying source function. The file browser parses the source on initial pick, but a later `var` click never re-asks the source whether it actually accepts varargs.

**There's a placeholder for the right answer** in 217's `## Future development ideas` section — "Language-spec-driven variadic detection." Each language spec would expose `parse_function_signature` or similar that tells the editor "this function is variadic on parameter N" — and the editor would refuse to toggle `var` on a non-variadic parameter (or auto-toggle it on a variadic one). 231 (move parsers into `langs/<name>/parser.js`) is the natural prerequisite — once the per-language parser knows about variadicness, the editor just asks.

Want me to open a small ticket for "reject `var` toggle on non-variadic parameters" as a follow-up? Or fold it into 231's scope (since 231 is already moving the parsers around)?

### [1051] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:57:49.533Z

### [1052] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T17:58:12.883Z

Fair — every ticket is part of the system's permanent history, none are throwaway.

Let me answer the four questions in order.

**Why is the button in the editor instead of the spec?** Mostly history. 217 shipped before the phase-3 language-spec system was anything more than a stub. Per-language knowledge had to live somewhere, and "somewhere" at the time was the editor's JavaScript. Specifically: `assets/js/007-filebrowser.js` carries hard-coded `parse_lua` and `parse_bash` functions that walk function signatures and pull out parameter names. The `var` toggle is the editor's hedge against not actually knowing whether a parameter is variadic — it lets the user *assert* "this one is" instead of detecting it. Phase 3 (issues 301–317) is the build-out of the real spec system. Once that's there, the editor stops needing to ask the user.

**What does the right answer look like once a user is defining their own language spec?** Per the issue 303 / 307 / 308 contract, a language spec is a directory like `langs/<name>/` containing:

- `spec.c` — the runtime side. Defines a `lang_spec_t` struct (issue 303) with callbacks for `init`, `invoke`, `teardown`, etc. The runtime asks the spec to invoke a function with N arguments.
- `Makefile` — builds `spec.c` into a `.so` the pool loads at startup.
- `parser.js` *(this part is issue 231)* — the editor-side. An ES module exporting:

  ```js
  export function parse_function_signature(source_text, fn_name) {
    return {
      name: '…',
      inputs: ['sep', 'text'],
      variadic_inputs: ['text'],     // names whose slots can be many
      output: 'result',
    };
  }
  ```

That's the whole contract on the editor side. When the user picks a function in the file browser, the editor calls this parser. The result tells the inspector "here are the inputs, and *this one* is variadic." From there the variadic UI runs by itself — the `var` button is no longer a *toggle* the user has to set; it's a *fact* the editor already knows. For a non-variadic param the button is either hidden, disabled, or simply absent. For a variadic param the slots auto-grow without ever asking.

For a user adding their own language (Python, say): they write `langs/python/spec.c` for the runtime invocation, and `langs/python/parser.js` for the editor surface. The parser detects `*args` / `**kwargs` and reports the right `variadic_inputs`. No editor changes. No JavaScript-side patches in `007-filebrowser.js`. The whole language is one drop-in directory.

**Why isn't this part of 217?** 217 is complete and committed. Adding to its scope means re-opening it, which the project's immutability rule for completed tickets pushes against. The work also doesn't actually belong in 217's scope as 217 was framed — 217's *delivered* the variadic UI mechanic and was explicit that detection-from-source was deferred to "language-spec-driven variadic detection" as a future idea. That future is now 231's territory.

**What is 231?** "Move per-language signature parsers out of the file browser into `langs/<name>/parser.js`." Currently the editor's file browser has `parse_lua` and `parse_bash` hard-coded inside it — same way the variadic button is hard-coded as a manual toggle. 231 moves both pieces of per-language knowledge into the language's own directory, where they belong. The mechanical change is: cut `parse_lua` out of `007-filebrowser.js`, paste into `langs/lua/parser.js` as an ES module, change the file browser to dynamically import whichever parser matches the file extension. Once that lands:

- Adding a new language is one new directory.
- The parser's return value gets to grow new fields (`variadic_inputs`, `optional_inputs`, `output_type`, …) and the editor consumes them generically.
- The variadic-not-actually-variadic question collapses into "look at the spec, see if the param is in `variadic_inputs`, render accordingly."

So the answer to your earlier question isn't "small ticket" or "fold into 231." It's: **231 is the prerequisite.** Once 231 lands, the variadic-detection behavior falls out of it almost for free — the parsers already produce a `variadic_inputs` list, the editor just stops trusting the user to know better and trusts the parser instead. That's worth a paragraph inside 231 explicitly, not a separate ticket.

Want me to expand 231's spec to call out the variadic-correctness consequence and the corresponding inspector change?

/* langs/c/spec.c — C language spec for SoraMech.
 *
 * Per-worker handle is a small dlopen cache. `compile` runs gcc
 * to turn a box source file into a .so; `invoke` dlopens the .so
 * (caching the handle), dlsyms the named function, and calls it
 * through a fixed signature.
 *
 * Designed in issue 307. C box functions follow a single
 * convention that matches the spec's `invoke` shape minus the
 * per-worker handle:
 *
 *     int box_fn(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
 *                void *out_buf, int out_capacity, int *out_size);
 *
 * Return 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on failure. This iteration
 * does not generate per-box wrappers from declared signatures
 * (issue 307's optional advanced mode) — every C box uses the
 * fixed signature above. Wrapper generation can land as a follow-on
 * once we have a box whose declared types diverge from the byte-
 * array shape.
 */

#include "lang-spec.h"

#include <dlfcn.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <sys/wait.h>
#include <unistd.h>

#define C_CACHE_SIZE 32

/* {{{ Per-worker dlopen cache */
typedef struct {
    char *path;     /* strdup'd; identifies the cache entry         */
    void *dl;       /* dlopen handle                                */
} c_cache_entry_t;

typedef struct {
    c_cache_entry_t entries[C_CACHE_SIZE];
    int             count;
} c_handle_t;

typedef int (*c_box_fn_t)(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
                          void *out_buf, int out_capacity, int *out_size);
/* }}} */

/* {{{ c_init() */
static void *c_init(int worker_idx)
{
    (void)worker_idx;
    c_handle_t *h = calloc(1, sizeof *h);
    return h;
}
/* }}} */

/* {{{ c_teardown() */
static void c_teardown(void *handle)
{
    if (!handle) return;
    c_handle_t *h = (c_handle_t *)handle;
    for (int i = 0; i < h->count; i++) {
        if (h->entries[i].dl) dlclose(h->entries[i].dl);
        free(h->entries[i].path);
    }
    free(h);
}
/* }}} */

/* {{{ c_compile() — gcc -shared -fPIC */
static int c_compile(const char *src_path, const char *out_path)
{
    if (!src_path || !out_path) return -1;
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid < 0) return -1;
    if (pid == 0) {
        /* Child: replace ourselves with gcc. */
        execlp("gcc", "gcc",

/* src/011-spec-registry.h — language spec registry, public API.
 *
 * What it is, in a sentence: scans langs/<name>/ at startup,
 * dlopens every spec.so it finds, and exposes a name- and
 * extension-keyed lookup to the rest of the runtime.
 *
 * Designed in issue 303. The pool runner (issue 301) consults the
 * registry during per-worker init — for every language the map
 * uses, the worker's per-language handle is initialised via the
 * spec's `init` callback. The dispatch layer (issue 304) looks the
 * spec up at runtime to invoke a box's function.
 *
 * Lifetime: created at process startup, alive for the run, freed
 * at shutdown. dlclose() is called on every handle in destroy.
 *
 * Errors: spec_registry_load returns NULL and sets *err on failure
 * (cannot open langs_dir, dlopen failure, missing required symbol).
 * The caller frees *err.
 */

#ifndef SORAMECH_SPEC_REGISTRY_H
#define SORAMECH_SPEC_REGISTRY_H

#include "lang-spec.h"

#ifdef __cplusplus
extern "C" {
#endif

typedef struct spec_registry spec_registry_t;

/* Open every langs_dir/<name>/spec.so and read its
 * soramech_lang_spec symbol. Returns NULL on failure with *err set. */
spec_registry_t *spec_registry_load(const char *langs_dir, char **err);

/* dlclose every handle, free the registry. Safe on NULL. */
void             spec_registry_destroy(spec_registry_t *r);

int              spec_registry_size(const spec_registry_t *r);

---
/* langs/lua/spec.c — Lua language spec for SoraMech.
 *
 * Per-worker handle is a `lua_State` with the standard libraries
 * opened. Invocation convention: the box's source file is expected
 * to return a Lua table (`return M` at the bottom), and the
 * fn_name is a key into that table. Inputs arrive as byte arrays;
 * each one is pushed as a Lua string. The function's single return
 * value is converted to a string (via tostring semantics) and
 * memcpy'd into the caller's output buffer.
 *
 * Designed in issue 306. Implementation choices that matter:
 *
 *  - One lua_State per worker thread. lua_State is not
 *    thread-safe, but per-thread requires no locking.
 *  - The state's stack is rewound to its baseline depth at every
 *    invoke entry/exit so cross-call leaks don't accumulate.
 *  - File caching (luaL_loadfile once, replayed via lua_call) is
 *    deferred — this iteration loads the chunk on every invoke.
 *    Lands as a follow-on once profiling justifies it.
 *  - The chunk's side effects run on every invoke too (since
 *    we re-load and re-execute). Box source files should be pure
 *    "return M" modules for now.
 */

#include "lang-spec.h"

#include <lua.h>
#include <lauxlib.h>
#include <lualib.h>

### [1070] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-19T18:04:01.968Z

Here's how it works, walking from outside in.

**The contract is one C struct.** `langs/lang-spec.h` declares `lang_spec_t` — a struct of function pointers. A language spec is "an instance of this struct, compiled into a `.so`, dropped at `langs/<name>/spec.so`." That's the whole interface. Anyone adding a language fills the struct out and ships the directory.

The fields in order of when they fire:

```
init(worker_idx)        — at pool startup, once per worker thread.
                           Returns an opaque handle the spec wants
                           to carry around (Lua's lua_State, C's
                           dlopen cache, Bash's persistent socket).
compile(src, out)       — optional. For languages that need a build
                           step, called once per source file. C
                           runs gcc -shared -fPIC. Lua and Bash
                           leave this NULL.
invoke(handle, file, fn,
       inputs, sizes, n,
       out, cap, *out_size)
                        — per box call. The runtime hands you bytes
                           in, you write bytes out.
teardown(handle)        — at pool shutdown, per worker.
```

Plus a few fast-path / bridge callbacks from 312 (`invoke_native`, `invoke_json`, `native_to_json`, `json_to_native`) that are optional — leave them NULL and dispatch falls back to the canonical `invoke`.

**Discovery happens at startup.** `src/011-spec-registry.c` scans `langs/*/`, `dlopen`s each `spec.so`, reads the exported `soramech_lang_spec` symbol (the struct), and builds a name-and-extension-keyed lookup. The pool runner consults that registry per-worker to call `init`. The dispatch layer consults it per-task to call `invoke`.

**How "what you define" turns into the call — today.** This is the part the existing implementation does the *least* automatic glue for. Three pieces:

1. **The spec author writes plain C.** No glue generation. They write `static int my_invoke(...)` and assign it to `.invoke = my_invoke` in the exported struct. Same for `init`, `teardown`, etc. The "spec" *is* the hand-written C file.

2. **The box author follows a per-language convention.** This is where you'd expect glue and where there mostly isn't any yet:
   - **Lua box**: write a file that does `local M = {}; function M.foo(a, b) ... end; return M`. The Lua spec's invoke pushes each input as a Lua string, calls `M[fn_name](...)`, and writes the return value back out. The convention is "return a table."
   - **C box**: write a function with this *fixed signature*:
     ```c
     int my_fn(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
               void *out_buf, int out_capacity, int *out_size);
     ```
     The signature matches the spec's `invoke` shape minus the per-worker handle. Compile-time, the spec's `compile` runs gcc on the file; invoke-time, it dlsyms the symbol and calls it. **No wrapper generation today** — every C box reads its bytes by hand.
   - **Bash box**: a function `my_fn() { ... }` in a `.sh`. The Bash spec's invoke runs the function via a persistent socketpair (issue 308) and reads stdout.

3. **The glue is the `invoke` callback itself.** That callback's body is the user-facing translation layer between "bytes on a wire" and "a call into the language." Each language's spec.c hand-codes it. For Lua: push, call, tostring, copy. For C: dlsym, call function pointer, return. For Bash: serialize, send over socket, read.

**Where "generated glue" *will* matter — the typed wrapper from 307.** This is the piece that doesn't exist yet but is the natural next move. The C spec's comment calls it out (langs/c/spec.c lines 16–19): "does not generate per-box wrappers from declared signatures (issue 307's optional advanced mode) — every C box uses the fixed signature above." The vision is:

- The user writes `int my_add(int a, int b) { return a + b; }` — a plain C function with declared types.
- A build step generates a wrapper file: `int my_add_wrapper(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n, void *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size) { int a = *(int*)inputs[0]; int b = *(int*)inputs[1]; int r = my_add(a, b); *(int*)out_buf = r; *out_size = sizeof(int); return 0; }`.
- The user never sees the wrapper.

That wrapper file is the only place "glue" gets *generated* in the design as drafted. Everywhere else, the glue is hand-written inside the spec's `invoke`. And the wrapper-generation step itself is also hand-written in the C spec — the spec's `compile` callback would emit the wrapper from a signature it parsed from the source.

**Where 217's variadic-detection fits.** Two layers, neither of which involves code generation:

- **Editor side (issue 231)**: `langs/<name>/parser.js` exposes `parse_function_signature(source, fn_name)` returning `{inputs, variadic_inputs, ...}`. The editor's file browser and inspector consume it. This is pure JavaScript ES-module per language; no C, no runtime, no glue.
- **Runtime side (already works)**: the spec's `invoke` receives `n_inputs` and `inputs[]` of whatever length the dispatch layer assembled. For Lua's `function M.foo(sep, ...)`, the spec pushes all N as Lua values and the `...` collects the tail naturally. For Bash, `"$@"` does the same. For C with the fixed-signature convention, `inputs[]` is just an N-element array — the user's box function reads as many as it cares about.

So **adding a new language is one directory:**

```
langs/python/
  spec.c          ← lang_spec_t with init/invoke/teardown for Python
  Makefile        ← builds spec.c into spec.so against libpython
  parser.js       ← parses def fn(*args): … and tells the editor
```

The runtime sees the new `spec.so` at startup and registers it. The editor sees the new `parser.js` and uses it for `.py` files. The user never edits the editor or the dispatch layer.

For the variadic-correctness question: it's purely editor-side. When 217 is reopened, the work is "after 231 lands, parser.js's `parse_function_signature` already produces `variadic_inputs`; the inspector consumes it and renders `var` differently for variadic-by-the-language vs not." Zero runtime change.

Does that map cleanly onto what you wanted to understand? If a specific layer is still murky I can drill in.
